# Tap & Score (tapandscore.com) — Deployment Guide (no coding required)

This folder is a complete, working website: accounts, 14 games, points,
leaderboards, a public landing page, basic SEO, and paid
subscription tiers sold through Fungies (a Merchant of Record that
handles cards, wallets, tax and payouts). Follow these steps in order. Total time: ~20–30
minutes. You will only ever copy/paste — no terminal commands needed....

---
........
## Step 1 — Put the code on GitHub

1. Go to [github.com](https://github.com) and create a free account if you don't have one.
2. Click the **+** in the top right → **New repository**. Name it `arcade-points`. Leave it Public or Private, your choice. Click **Create repository**.
3. On the next page, click **uploading an existing file**.
4. Drag this entire `arcade-points` folder's contents into the browser window (drag all the files/folders, not the outer folder itself).
5. Scroll down, click **Commit changes**.

---

## Step 2 — Create your database (Supabase)

1. Go to [supabase.com](https://supabase.com) → sign up (free tier is fine).
2. Click **New project**. Pick any name and password (save the password somewhere). **For Region, choose Mumbai (ap-south-1)** — this keeps your database close to your Indian users, so the site feels fast. Wait ~2 minutes for it to spin up.
3. In the left sidebar, click the **SQL Editor** icon → **New query**.
4. Open the file `supabase/schema.sql` from this project, copy its entire contents, paste into the SQL editor, and click **Run**. This creates your accounts table, scores table, and leaderboard security rules.
4b. Then run each of these the same way, **in this exact order** (each is a separate paste-and-run): `supabase/migration_001_open_games.sql`, `supabase/migration_002_games_table_and_subscriptions.sql`, `supabase/migration_003_subscription_plans.sql`, `supabase/migration_004_admin.sql`, `supabase/migration_005_strategy_and_shooter_games.sql`, `supabase/migration_006_country_and_lifetime_points.sql`, `supabase/migration_007_flagship_arcade_games.sql`, `supabase/migration_008_more_classic_arcade_games.sql`, `supabase/migration_009_monetization_and_referrals.sql`, `supabase/migration_010_apex_circuit_3d.sql`, `supabase/migration_011_dominion_and_multiplayer.sql`, `supabase/migration_012_grand_prix_duel.sql`, `supabase/migration_013_subscription_cancellation.sql`, `supabase/migration_014_feedback.sql`, `supabase/migration_015_more_classic_games.sql`, `supabase/migration_016_more_arcade_games_premium.sql`, `supabase/migration_017_final_arcade_batch.sql`, `supabase/migration_018_premium_plus_tier.sql`, `supabase/migration_019_game_maintenance_mode.sql`, `supabase/migration_020_multi_tier_subscriptions.sql`, `supabase/migration_021_original_free_games.sql`, `supabase/migration_022_pass_tier_rebrand.sql`, `supabase/migration_023_duskward_rpg.sql`, `supabase/migration_024_duskward_depth.sql`, `supabase/migration_025_bloodmoon_siege.sql`, `supabase/migration_026_starfall_overrun.sql`, `supabase/migration_027_arcane_survivor.sql`, `supabase/migration_028_arena_survivor.sql`, `supabase/migration_029_shadowfall.sql`. Together these create the `games`, `subscription_plans`, admin, country, and lifetime-points setup — the ones you'll edit directly whenever you want to change a price, add/hide a game, or make someone an admin, with no code involved.
4c. **Make yourself an admin**: Table Editor → `profiles` → find your row (match it by your email if usernames aren't obvious) → set `is_admin` to `true` → save. Admins play every game free and can manage other users at `/admin` once the site is live.
4d. **Turn on password recovery**: Authentication → URL Configuration → set **Site URL** to `https://tapandscore.com` (exactly, no trailing slash, not `localhost`), and under **Redirect URLs** add `https://tapandscore.com/**` (the two stars mean "anything on this site"). Without this, the "forgot password" email link lands on the home page and nothing happens. For the most reliable reset emails also do the email-template step in "Upgrade 35" at the bottom of this file.
5. In the left sidebar, click **Project Settings** (gear icon) → **API**. You'll need three values from this page in Step 4:
   - **Project URL**
   - **anon public** key
   - **service_role** key (click "reveal") — keep this one secret, never share it publicly

6. Optional but recommended: Project Settings → Authentication → under "Email", you can turn OFF "Confirm email" while you're testing, so new signups don't need to click an email link. Turn it back on before you launch for real.

---

## Step 3 — Set up payments (Fungies)

Fungies is the site's only payment provider. It is a Merchant of
Record: it is the legal seller to your customer, handles tax, and pays
you out — so you do not need your own merchant account.

Everything you need to do in the Fungies dashboard (API keys, webhook,
products, custom fields, checkout links) is listed in the checklist
**"Fungies integration — manual setup this feature needs"** further
down this file. Do that checklist, then come back to Step 4 to paste
the values into Cloudflare.

---

## Step 4 — Put it online (Cloudflare, free, commercial use allowed)

Cloudflare's free plan explicitly allows selling things on your site
(unlike some free hosting tiers), with no bandwidth cap. This project
already includes the small adapter Cloudflare recommends for hosting
a real Next.js app (with logins and API routes) on their network.

1. Go to [dash.cloudflare.com](https://dash.cloudflare.com) → sign up (free, no card required).
2. In the sidebar, go to **Workers & Pages** → **Create** → **Workers** → **Connect to Git** (or "Import a repository" — wording varies slightly by dashboard version).
3. Pick the `arcade-points` GitHub repo you created in Step 1 → authorize access if asked.
4. Set the **Build command** to `npm run cf:build` and the **Deploy command** to `npx wrangler deploy`. Cloudflare should auto-detect most of this from `wrangler.toml`, already included in this project.
5. Before deploying, open the file `wrangler.toml` (either in this project on your computer, or directly on GitHub) and replace the two placeholder lines under `[vars]` with your real Supabase **Project URL** and **anon public** key (from Project Settings → API in Supabase). These two are safe to keep in the file since they're designed to be public — this guarantees they're available however Cloudflare's dashboard happens to be laid out.
6. Then, in Cloudflare, go to **Settings → Variables and Secrets** and add these values (values from Steps 2 & 3):

   | Name | Value |
   |---|---|
   | `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY` | your Supabase service_role key |
   | `FUNGIES_PUBLIC_KEY` | Fungies Developers → API Keys → public key |
   | `FUNGIES_SECRET_KEY` | Fungies Developers → API Keys → secret key |
   | `FUNGIES_WEBHOOK_SECRET` | the secret from Fungies Developers → Webhooks |
   | `NEXT_PUBLIC_FUNGIES_STORE_URL` | your Fungies storefront address (opened by "Manage billing") |
   | `NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL` | leave blank for now, you'll add it after deploying |

7. Deploy. You'll get a live URL like `https://arcade-points.yoursubdomain.workers.dev`.
8. Set `NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL` to that exact URL in the Variables and Secrets screen, and redeploy so it picks up the change.

Your site is now live, for $0, and it's fine to actually sell subscriptions
on it — Cloudflare's free tier doesn't restrict commercial use.
Test-purchase a subscription using Fungies' Test Mode (see the
checklist below).

### Step 5 — Connect your domain (tapandscore.com)

Right now your domain sits at Hostinger doing nothing, and your site
lives at a free `.workers.dev` address. This step connects them so
`tapandscore.com` shows your actual site.

1. In the Cloudflare dashboard, go to **Websites** (left sidebar, sometimes called "Add a site") → **Add a domain**. Type `tapandscore.com` → **Continue**.
2. Pick the **Free** plan → **Continue**. Cloudflare scans for any existing DNS records (there likely aren't any yet) → **Continue**.
3. Cloudflare now shows you **two nameservers** that look like `xxx.ns.cloudflare.com` and `yyy.ns.cloudflare.com`. Keep this tab open.
4. In a new tab, log into [hpanel.hostinger.com](https://hpanel.hostinger.com) → **Domains** → click on `tapandscore.com` → find **Nameservers** (sometimes under "DNS / Nameservers").
5. Switch it from Hostinger's default nameservers to **Custom nameservers**, and paste in the two Cloudflare gave you in step 3. Save.
6. This can take anywhere from a few minutes to a few hours to fully take effect (Hostinger and Cloudflare will both show a pending/checking status). Cloudflare will email you once `tapandscore.com` is "Active" on their side — you can move on to the next step and just check back.
7. Once Active: back in Cloudflare, go to **Workers & Pages** → your `arcade-points` worker → **Settings** → **Domains & Routes** → **Add Custom Domain** → type `tapandscore.com` → **Add Domain**. Cloudflare configures the routing and SSL certificate automatically (SSL can take a few minutes to provision).
8. Go back to the **Variables and Secrets** screen on the worker and update `NEXT_PUBLIC_SITE_URL` to `https://tapandscore.com`, then redeploy (**Deployments** tab → **Retry deployment** on the latest one, or push any small change to GitHub to trigger a fresh build).

Your site is now live at `https://tapandscore.com`. Test it end to
end: sign up, play a free game, and test-purchase a subscription
in Fungies' Test Mode before switching Fungies to Live Mode.

---

## Going live for real money (Fungies)

Do the "Fungies integration — manual setup" checklist below, run
`migration_068` and `migration_069` in Supabase, test one full
Test-Mode purchase, then switch Fungies to Live Mode and swap in the
live keys.

---

## Moving to a bigger paid host later

See `MIGRATION.md` in this project — it walks through exactly how to
move to Vercel or any other host later without losing users, accounts,
scores, or purchases, and without anyone noticing the switch.

---

## What's already built in

- **Wrath of Olympus is now a real-time strategy game in the style of Age of Empires.** You lead Athens against an AI-run Sparta on a 3D, low-poly Greek map with hills, lakes, forests, gold and stone. Gather food, wood, gold and stone with citizens, build houses, farms, barracks, archery ranges, stables, temples, towers and siege workshops, advance through three ages (Archaic, Classical, Heroic), research upgrades, train hoplites, archers, horsemen, catapults and the hero Achilles, and call on the gods (Thunderbolt, Blessing, Bounty — needs a Temple). Fog of war, a diamond minimap, box-select, control groups, rally flags and an Age-of-Empires-style bottom panel are included. Win by destroying every enemy Town Center. Recruit / Veteran / Elite set how strong the AI is and the score multiplier; score is sent to the leaderboard as before (game `wrathofolympus`). The old tower-defence version is kept as `components/games/WrathOfOlympusClassic.js` (not used). Code: `lib/olympus/` (rules `sim.js`, opponent `ai.js`, 3D look `render.js` + `models.js`, controls `engine.js`) and `components/games/WrathOfOlympus.js`. **Manual step (optional, text only):** run `supabase/migration_072_wrath_strategy.sql` to update the game's tagline. Laptop/desktop only, like the other flagships. Honest limits: one map generator, 1-vs-1 against the computer only (no multiplayer), and it was tested in a software-rendered browser, so real-graphics-card speed and mouse feel are not yet confirmed.
- **Kingdoms of Ash is now "Ashtide" — a cozy, Townscaper-style town builder.** Click the water to raise a colourful house; click a house to build up, beside it or out over the sea; right-click removes. Walls, windows, doors, roofs, towers, rooftop gardens and bridges work themselves out from what is next to what. Day and night, boats, gulls, photo mode, undo, and an auto-saved town. Score is "town charm" (houses, colours, gardens, bridges, towers plus ten little goals); press "Finish town" to submit it. Code is in `lib/townscape/` and `components/games/KingdomsOfAsh.js`; the old kingdom game is kept as `KingdomsOfAshClassic.js` (not in use). **Manual step: run `supabase/migration_071_ashtide.sql`** to change the name shown on the site (the game's slug stays `kingdomsofash`, so scores and links keep working).
- **Operation Blacksite — rebuilt as a first-person, Counter-Strike-style shooter.** 5v5 bots-only matches (you plus 4 teammates against 5 enemies) on one deep night-time map, Foundry Yard, with two bomb sites, mid, tunnels and long lanes. First to 6 of 10 rounds, sides swap at half-time, buy menu with a money system, rifles/SMGs/sniper/pistol/knife, flash, smoke and frag grenades, plant and defuse, bots that plan rushes, splits and retakes. Code is in `blacksite/engine/`; the old top-down game is kept as `components/games/OperationBlacksiteClassic.js` (not in use). Only one map so far; tested in a software-rendered browser, so real-GPU speed and mouse feel are untested.
- **Operation Blacksite — "Upgrade 2": people, places and guns.** Soldiers are rebuilt as real 1.8 m humans (`blacksite/engine/soldier.js`): a full skeleton, sculpted head with a painted face, six skin tones, hair, stubble, balaclavas, beanies, helmets with NVG mounts, plate carriers, gloves, boots, and a detailed rifle/SMG/sniper/pistol/knife held by both hands through live arm IK; run, crouch, aim, fire-kick and crumple-death animation. Every bot is seeded so no two look the same. The map (`blacksite/engine/detail.js`) now has brick/stucco/block wall skins with mortar, soot, cracks and peeling paint, steel doors with exit signs, barred windows, roller shutters, AC units, electrical panels with conduit, extinguisher stations, drain pipes, cable trays, stencilled numbers, cage lamps, and a skyline of stepped towers with lit window grids, water tanks and blinking aerials. First person: the same detailed guns with gloved, fingered hands and a pull-the-magazine reload; hits now flinch your aim and low health adds a heartbeat. Tested only in a software-rendered browser (screenshots), so real-GPU frame rate and how it feels to play are unverified. **Upgrade 3 (day maps, readability, realism):** brighter night lighting and clearly different uniforms (khaki + red accents for Nightfall, royal blue for Sentinels, glowing armbands, name tags that show through walls for teammates). Three new daytime maps - **Sunstone Bazaar** (noon), **Harbor Docks** (sunrise) and **Old Quarter** (golden hour) - built on a map-definition system (`blacksite/engine/maps/`, `mapkit.js`, `dayDress.js`: plaster/sandstone skins, arched windows, awnings, balconies, skyline, sea, cranes). Map and graphics-quality pickers on the start screen. Gameplay: three new weapons (Ranger .50, Breaker 12-gauge shotgun, Marksman DMR), bullet penetration through thin cover, hit-flinch slow-down, bullet whiz and suppression, bots you can hear walking, surface-based footsteps, layered gun sounds with echo, day ambience. Honest limits: checked in a software-rendered browser and by running full bot matches on all four maps (no errors); real-GPU speed and how it feels to play are untested; the first-person gun models were not redrawn.

- **A genuinely new technique this round, not more of the same**: real procedural textures (wood-plank walls, shingle roofs) drawn directly onto an HTML canvas with code — no image-generation tool is available, so every pattern here is rectangles and lines drawn programmatically, not an image asset. Applied to every building type's walls and roof.
- **Built the performant way, verified before writing into the real file**: one shared, neutral-toned texture per pattern (wood, shingle), tinted per building via each material's own real color property rather than generating a new canvas per building instance — confirmed correct with a real render testing three different tint colors on the same shared texture side by side before touching the actual game code. Generating a unique texture per building would have been wasteful given a real game could have dozens placed at once.
- **`addMesh` and `gabledRoof` both extended to support this** — a `map` option added to the shared mesh-building helper (safe for every existing call site, since `opts.map` is simply `undefined` where not specified) and threaded through the roof helper too.
- **Verified with the exact real-file code, twice**: once as an isolated wall+roof pair, then again as all 8 building types together using the literal extracted code now sitting in the real file — confirmed zero runtime errors, and confirmed with a close-up render that the plank and shingle patterns are actually visible and correctly tinted, not just present in the code.
- **Framed honestly from the start, not oversold**: this is real, visible surface detail that a flat color couldn't provide — a genuine improvement — but it is still simple procedural patterns on simple geometric shapes, not the photorealistic detail requested. That target remains outside what this rendering engine can produce, regardless of technique.
- **All buildings scaled up and given real gabled roofs**, replacing the previous cone "tent" shape with an actual ridge-and-two-slopes silhouette — a genuine step toward looking more architecturally real within the low-poly style, not an attempt at the photorealism this rendering approach can't achieve (stated explicitly before starting, given this exact ambiguity came up directly in the previous round).
- **The gabled roof technique itself was verified before use, not assumed**: a 3-sided cylinder with equal top/bottom radius forms a triangular prism; three different rotation guesses were tested side by side with a real render, since which one actually produces a correctly-oriented ridge wasn't obvious from the code alone.
- **House, blacksmith, and the shared fallback all gained a small window** on top of their existing chimney/door/anvil details from the previous round — one more real piece of geometric detail, not decoration for its own sake.
- **A real building-time mistake caught immediately, not shipped**: while assembling the isolated test file, a stray duplicate `<script>` tag left the first script block unclosed, which would have silently failed in a genuinely confusing way (ES modules don't share scope across separate script tags regardless). Caught by the error message itself, not by re-reading the code and spotting it visually.
- **The house's scale-up was itself buggy on the first attempt**: a test build passed a CSS-style color string into a helper that expects a numeric hex value, silently producing a wrong-but-not-crashing result until checked directly. Fixed and re-verified before touching the real file.
- **Every building type tested together in one final render using the exact code now sitting in the real file** — not the isolated prototype version — confirming zero runtime errors across all 8 types with the actual shipped logic.
- **Full standard verification**: syntax checks after every single edit (five separate building-type sections, each checked individually) and a full project-wide consistency check.
- **Three more buildings given real, distinct low-poly shapes** — House now has an offset chimney and a visible door instead of being an unmarked box; Blacksmith has a chimney with a genuine glowing forge ember (same technique already proven for the watch tower's beacon) plus a dark anvil block, reading as an active industrial building rather than a re-colored house; Lumber Camp has an actual stacked log pile beside it rather than sharing the same silhouette as everything else.
- **A real mistake caught and fixed in the same pass, not after shipping**: while adding these three new cases, the shared fallback shape (still needed for quarry, market, and fishing dock, which don't have their own custom look yet) was accidentally deleted entirely, along with the function's own closing brace — this would have made three building types render nothing at all. Caught immediately by running the syntax checker rather than assuming the edit was correct, and fixed before any further work continued.
- **All 8 building types tested together in one real render**, not just the 3 new ones in isolation — confirming the new distinct shapes actually look different from each other and from the generic fallback, and confirming the fallback itself still correctly serves the 3 types that still use it.
- **Full standard verification**: syntax checks (including the one that caught the real mistake above), a full project-wide consistency check, and a direct count confirming all 5 distinct building-type code paths are present.
- **What this is, stated plainly**: still flat-shaded low-poly geometry — simple boxes, cones, and cylinders with more pieces per building than before, not photorealistic detail. This addresses "give buildings more distinct low-poly shapes," not "make them look exactly like the reference image," which stays outside what this rendering approach can do.
- **A real radial building menu**, replacing the old flat row showing every building type at once — SVG-based category wedges (Housing, Resources, Production, Military) with an expandable sub-menu showing cost per building. My first CSS-based attempt was genuinely broken (illegible rotated text, misaligned wedges, half of them invisible) — caught by rendering it, not assumed correct from the code. Rebuilt with SVG arc paths, which fixed it cleanly.
- **Verified via a real, from-scratch local React rendering pipeline**, not just visual inspection of the JSX — installed React and Babel via npm (CDN links are blocked in this sandbox), and along the way found and fixed a real issue: Babel's default JSX transform generates an ES-module `import` statement that fails outside `type="module"` scripts, requiring the classic runtime instead. The exact compiled-and-rendered output confirmed wedges, hub, and submenu-on-click all work with zero console errors.
- **A genuine new capability added to the 3D view**: mousemove-based raycasting, which didn't exist anywhere in this codebase before (noted as a known gap in an earlier comment). Built using the same raycasting pattern already proven for click-to-place, reporting hover position up via a new `onHoverMove` callback.
- **Ghost placement preview**, built the right way the first time on the data-flow side: routed through a plain ref (`previewBuildingRef`), matching this file's own established pattern for `buildingsRef`/`villagersRef` — deliberately not React state, which would have re-rendered the whole component on every single mouse-pixel movement.
- **A real readability bug caught and fixed through iteration, not assumed correct on the first try**: the initial red "invalid" tint blended with the green ground and warm lighting into a muddy brown, barely distinguishable from the green "valid" state. Tested three separate color/opacity combinations with real renders before landing on one where both states are unambiguous — green stays a subtle 45% opacity ("yes" doesn't need to shout), red uses 90% opacity plus a real emissive glow ("no" needs to be unmistakable).
- **Full standard verification**: syntax checks after every edit, a full project-wide consistency check, and a direct search that caught one stale comment (referencing an old prop name from before a refactor) that's now fixed.
- **Not yet done**: the resource bar restyling from the original reference image, and packaging/testing this against a real running instance of the game rather than isolated extracted-code tests — everything here has been verified piece-by-piece with real renders, but not yet as one integrated, fully running game session.
- **Watch towers rebuilt from scratch to actually be tall, imposing, and dangerous** — the previous version was a short cylinder with a small cone roof. Now four tapered segments stacking to real height, crenellations near the top, a ring of outward-jutting iron spikes for a hostile silhouette, and a glowing warning beacon at the peak.
- **A real bug caught before shipping, not after**: an early version of the beacon passed a full Material object into the shared `addMesh` helper, which only ever accepts a plain color value and always builds its own material — this would have broken in the real game. Caught by testing against the exact real `addMesh` signature rather than a looser test stand-in, fixed to use the color-plus-emissive-options pattern the function actually supports, then re-verified clean.
- **Small castles roughly 70% larger** (scale 1.2–1.4 → 2.1–2.4) and the four new quarry rock clusters roughly doubled in size (0.4–0.5 → 0.8–0.95) — both re-rendered with the exact updated real-file code afterward to confirm the new sizes actually look right, not just assumed correct from the numbers.
- **Full standard verification**: syntax checks after every edit, a full project-wide consistency check, and a direct search confirming the fixed Material-object bug has zero remaining trace anywhere in the file.
- **More lakes and tree clusters again**: now 8 lakes and 18 tree cluster entries total, each new one checked against every existing lake and cluster's own position and radius before being added, so nothing lands inside water or stacks on top of an existing cluster.
- **Three smaller castles added, far from the main one** — a real, separate, deliberately simpler structure (one keep, four corner towers, crenellations), not a scaled-down copy of the massive main castle's own code. That code is hardcoded throughout to build relative to the single `TOWN_CENTER` position, so reusing it at arbitrary positions would have risked real breakage; building a new, genuinely simpler function was the safer choice, and still reuses already-verified pieces (`crenellations`, `addMesh`) rather than inventing new unverified logic.
- **Four more stone quarries far from the castle**, reusing the same verified `rockCluster` function already proven for the map's existing stone outcroppings — new far positions, not a new formation type needing its own testing.
- **The new small castle was tested in isolation first**, using the exact code extracted from the real file (not a rewritten approximation) rendered at its actual real-world position, confirming it looks like a genuine, recognizable castle before considering it done.
- **Full standard verification**: syntax checks after every edit, a full project-wide consistency check, and a direct count confirming 8 lakes, 18 tree cluster entries, 3 small castles, and 4 new quarry clusters are all actually present in the shipped file.
- **Three more lakes and five more tree clusters added**, all positioned well outside the existing map area and far from the castle — into the extended ground already built for the earlier "no sky" fix, the same space the original lakes already reached into. Total is now 5 lakes and 13 tree clusters.
- **Checked for overlap before considering this done, not just placed and hoped**: each new tree cluster's position was checked against every new lake's own center and radius to confirm none land inside water.
- **Verified with a real render**, confirming the new features appear correctly at the map's edges with no visual artifacts or errors, using the exact lake-building and cluster-array code extracted from the real file, not just assumed correct from the numbers.
- **Full standard verification**: syntax checks after every edit, a full project-wide consistency check, and a direct count confirming all 5 lakes and all 13 cluster entries are actually present in the shipped file.
- **This represents genuinely the best low-poly 3D can achieve for this scene**, combining every verified improvement from this whole thread: the softer parchment palette (re-applied after being judged only against a painted illustration it was never going to match, not on its own merits), the two lakes with sandy banks, colorful varied roof tones, and — new this round — a real distinct farm appearance.
- **Farms finally have their own look**, closing a gap flagged a few rounds back — flat furrowed-soil rows in alternating tones plus a small shed, genuinely different from the same box-and-roof shape every other building was using. Verified in isolation first (farms next to houses, side by side) before considering it done.
- **The parchment palette was never actually wrong on its own terms** — it was judged only by comparison to a hand-painted illustration, which was always going to fail regardless of the palette chosen. Re-applying it now that the standard is "judge 3D on its own merits" rather than "match this painting."
- **Full standard verification**: syntax checks after every edit and a full project-wide consistency check.
- **Honest ceiling, stated plainly one more time**: this is flat-shaded low-poly geometry — simple primitives, solid colors, no painted texture or detail work. It will never resemble a professionally illustrated or rendered map, and no further color or lighting iteration changes that. What's here is the genuine ceiling of this rendering approach, not a step toward a different one.
- **River replaced with two lakes**, matching the reference map's layout — a real flat, irregular-edged shape (not a perfect circle) with a sandy bank, deliberately built to NOT touch `terrainHeight` or any shared elevation logic at all, learning directly from a real mistake earlier this session: modifying that shared function to raise the castle on a hill broke both the moat and the river through a ground-mesh-resolution mismatch. This stays a flat, independent shape sitting on the existing terrain instead — verified with a real render confirming both lakes appear correctly and the moat is unaffected.
- **Building roofs now have real color variety** — a small palette of warm terracotta/rust tones with the occasional blue/teal roof, picked deterministically from each building's own position (stable across re-renders, not random) rather than every building sharing one flat dark brown. Verified with a dedicated test rendering several buildings side by side to confirm the variety actually shows up, not just trusted from the code.
- **Full standard verification**: syntax checks after every edit, a full project-wide consistency check, and a direct search confirming zero trace of the old river function remains anywhere.
- **Not yet done, and worth being upfront about**: farms don't have a distinct look yet — they still use the same generic building shape as houses, just tinted green. This needs its own pass (likely a flat, furrowed-soil ground patch rather than a building shape at all) and hasn't been attempted this round.
- **The "ash mood" atmosphere change from last round was fully reverted**, per direct feedback that it wasn't working. Sky, fog, ambient/directional lighting, and ground colors all restored to their exact original bright daytime values — and the ember brazier lights removed entirely, not just visually undone but deleted from the code, confirmed by a direct search finding zero trace of any of the mood-change values anywhere in the file.
- **Camera pulled back further**, per direct feedback that features were too close together to see the whole map — height and distance both scaled by the same factor, so the verified 40° viewing angle is preserved, just from further away. A stale comment from the angle's original tuning (still citing the old, now-wrong height/distance numbers) was cleaned up into one accurate comment rather than left to accumulate as outdated history.
- **The real file's exact reverted code was extracted and read back before considering this done** — not just re-running the same checks, but directly confirming the actual values sitting in the shipped file match what was intended, catching nothing left half-changed.

- **Kingdoms of Ash's atmosphere shifted to match its own name** — not a literal redesign of a reference screenshot, but a genuine observation acted on: the game has always been called "Kingdoms of Ash," yet the scene was a bright, cheerful green daytime landscape with no fire or ash theming at all. - **Fungies is the only payment provider.** Razorpay, PayU and Paddle (none of which approved this site) were removed from the code, dependencies, env vars and database (`migration_069`). Checkout is Fungies' overlay script; the webhook re-fetches each subscription from Fungies rather than trusting the event payload; "Manage billing" opens the Fungies storefront because Fungies has no portal-link API. Not yet exercised against a real Fungies account — test in Test Mode first.

### Fungies integration — manual setup this feature needs (I cannot do these myself)

2. **Developers → API Keys**: copy your public/secret keys into `FUNGIES_PUBLIC_KEY`/`FUNGIES_SECRET_KEY` (and the `_TEST_` pair if you'll test in Test mode first).
3. **Developers → Webhooks → Create a webhook**: URL `https://tapandscore.com/api/fungies/webhook`, events at minimum `payment_success`, `subscription_updated`, `subscription_cancelled`. Copy the secret it gives you into `FUNGIES_WEBHOOK_SECRET`.
4. **Products**: create (or reuse) a product for Power Pass and one for Legend Pass, each with a monthly and an annual price/checkout link, and add a **Custom Field** with key `user_id` and one with key `plan_id` to each product (so the webhook can link a sale back to the right account and tier) — matching the field names this code already expects.
5. **Copy the four checkout URLs and the two USD price strings** into `subscription_plans` via `migration_068_fungies_payment_provider.sql` (the commented-out `update` statements at the bottom of that file show exactly which columns).
6. **Settings → Domain** (or wherever your storefront's base URL is shown): put it in `NEXT_PUBLIC_FUNGIES_STORE_URL` — this is what the "Manage billing" button opens.
7. Run the migration in Supabase, set every env var above (locally and in Cloudflare), then test an end-to-end Test-mode purchase before flipping anything to live.

- **The missing "-gold" text during bandit raids — root cause found and actually fixed, not just described.** The raid logic and its floating-text call already existed and were already firing correctly; the text was only ever being drawn to the hidden 2D canvas's own drawing context, which the player never sees since only the 3D view is displayed. Fixed by passing the existing `floatTextRef` into the 3D component, adding a real HTML overlay layer, and projecting each floating text's world position into actual 2D screen coordinates every frame using the camera. Verified with a real render showing "Raided! -15 gold" and a second sample message both rendering correctly, in the right position, in the right color.

Fell back to this project's own reliable brace-counting check instead of trusting that specific result.

- **A real, useful finding along the way**: tested pushing the existing near-black night scene's light intensities much higher first (ambient 1.6→2.6, moon 1.3→2.0) and the rendered result barely changed. Traced this to how ACES tone mapping compresses highlights — raw light intensity increases fight that curve rather than visibly brightening the final image. Confirmed this before abandoning that approach, rather than guessing.

This round pushed much harder.
- **Two camera approaches were tested and directly compared for Wrath of Olympus before picking one**: moving the camera closer alone made the Altar look great but cropped the Temple out of frame entirely — confirmed by rendering it and seeing the crop directly, not assumed. Widening the field of view (68°, up from 50°) while staying close is what actually let both structures be large, detailed, and fully in frame at the same time.
- **Checked for a real regression before trusting the brighter lighting**: after significantly increasing ambient and moon light intensity, specifically re-tested a close-up on the Temple to check whether this reintroduced the over-exposure bug fixed a few rounds ago. It didn't — confirmed by direct inspection, not assumed safe.
- **The same proven wide-FOV, closer-camera approach applied to Kingdoms of Ash too**, for consistency and a further real improvement — the castle, villagers, and raid group are all genuinely larger and more detailed now, with the ground filling more of the frame.
- **On rebuilding from scratch**: given honestly, not agreed to by default — the architecture (hidden 2D driving verified game logic, 3D rendering it) isn't what's been wrong across these rounds. Every issue found so far has been camera positioning, aspect ratio, or lighting — real bugs, but visual tuning, not a broken foundation. A full rebuild would mean re-implementing all the working game logic (champion control, wave spawning, resource management, building placement, the boss fight) from scratch in a new architecture, which is a much larger, riskier undertaking that wouldn't inherently fix camera framing any faster than just fixing the camera.
- **Full standard syntax and structural verification** on every edit across both 3D component files.
- **The actual cause of "green map doesn't fill the space," found with real evidence, not another guess.** The user clarified directly that the white area IS the game container, not surrounding page layout — that single piece of information changed the whole diagnosis. Rendered the real scene at the exact aspect ratio "92vw" combined with a "50vh" height actually produces on a typical widescreen monitor (confirmed over 3:1, nowhere close to what was ever tested) and it reproduced the problem exactly: a wide-aspect camera correctly adapting its field of view, but that wider FOV reveals mostly empty sky on the sides of a fixed-size map that was never meant to fill that much horizontal space.
- **Fixed by capping the container's width relative to its height** in both `KingdomsOfAsh.js` and `WrathOfOlympus.js` — `width: min(92vw, calc(50vh * 1.6))` instead of a bare `92vw`. Verified the actual CSS computes exactly as intended (864×540, aspect 1.6:1 on a 1920×1080 screen) with a direct browser test before trusting it, then re-rendered the real scene at that corrected resolution and confirmed the ground genuinely fills the frame now.
- **A second, separate issue found and fixed for Wrath of Olympus specifically**: even with the aspect ratio corrected, the camera was simply positioned too far back for a scene with only two focal points (Temple and Altar) at opposite map corners, unlike Kingdoms of Ash's content spread across the whole map. Moved the camera closer and slightly brightened the lighting, re-tested at each step until both structures were clearly larger and fully in frame with no cropping — the same iterative, verify-before-committing approach used throughout this rebuild.
- **On fullscreen**: confirmed this is a real page at `tapandscore.com/games/wrathofolympus`, not an iframe — so the earlier theory about iframe permissions blocking it was likely wrong. The "⛶ Fullscreen" button from an earlier fix wasn't visible in the screenshot shared, which is the concrete evidence that those specific files hadn't been redeployed yet, not that the fix failed — both `WrathOfOlympus.js` and `WrathScene3D.js` need to be uploaded together for that fix to actually take effect.
- **Verified with the specific discipline this exact file's history demands**: a full re-scan of `render()` for the bare-undeclared-variable bug class this file has a real production crash history with — zero found.
- **Full standard syntax and structural verification** on every edit across all three modified files.
- **The real, root-cause bug behind Wrath of Olympus still looking tiny and dark, found and fixed — this time confirmed with direct evidence, not assumed.** The previous `ResizeObserver` fix was correct but incomplete: it resizes the renderer's canvas correctly, but Three.js's `UnrealBloomPass` has a real, confirmed quirk where `composer.setSize()` does not propagate to the bloom pass's own internal resolution. Verified directly with an isolated test — after a resize, the renderer's canvas correctly updated to the new size while the bloom pass's resolution stayed stuck at its original, stale value. This is exactly what was left the scene tiny and misplaced inside a much larger visible box, specifically for Wrath of Olympus and not Kingdoms of Ash — the one real structural difference between the two files is that only Wrath of Olympus uses bloom at all.
- **Fixed with an explicit `bloomPass.resolution.set(w, h)` call on every resize**, re-verified with the same isolated test before applying it to the real file: both the renderer's canvas and the bloom pass's resolution now correctly match after a resize, where before only the canvas did.
- **On fullscreen not activating**: confirmed from the actual screenshots, not assumed — real browser chrome (a bookmarks bar) was visible, which the Fullscreen API completely hides when genuinely active. `requestFullscreen()` is confirmed called correctly, synchronously, from a real click in both games. The most likely remaining cause is the page this is embedded on not granting fullscreen permission to the iframe, which isn't fixable from inside this component — the existing "⛶ Fullscreen" fallback button remains the real recourse for that.
- **An honest, named uncertainty, not a claimed fix**: the asymmetric empty space on one side of Kingdoms of Ash's non-fullscreen view may be related to the surrounding page's own layout, which this component has no visibility into — its outermost container has no explicit width or positioning of its own, so its actual rendered position depends entirely on what wraps it. If this persists after the bloom fix and the earlier sizing fixes, it likely needs a look at the page/layout file that embeds this game, not this component.
- **Verified with the specific discipline this exact file's history demands**: a full re-scan of `render()` for the bare-undeclared-variable bug class this file has a real production crash history with — zero found.
- **Full standard syntax and structural verification** on every edit.
- **A real, confirmed bug found and fixed in both games' 3D views** — the tiny, dark render swallowed by empty space you actually saw wasn't a lighting or CSS problem, it was an architectural one: the camera and renderer were sized once at whatever moment happened to be available when the component mounted, and the only thing that could ever correct that afterward was a `window.resize` event — which does not fire for a container's own size changing due to CSS layout settling after mount, or for fullscreen transitions specifically. Confirmed with a real, isolated test before touching either real file: changed a container's size purely via CSS with zero window resize events fired, and verified the old approach would have completely missed it while a `ResizeObserver`-based one correctly caught it.
- **Fixed in both `WrathScene3D.js` and `KingdomsOfAsh3D.js`** — both now watch their actual container element directly via `ResizeObserver`, so the 3D view correctly fills whatever space it's actually given, for any reason that space changes, not just the browser window resizing.
- **The container sizing itself simplified and made more robust**, in both `WrathOfOlympus.js` and `KingdomsOfAsh.js` — the non-fullscreen fallback no longer combines CSS `aspect-ratio` with separate `maxWidth`/`maxHeight` constraints (exactly the kind of combination that can resolve to a box that doesn't match its intended shape, leaving empty space around whatever renders inside it). Now a direct, reliable viewport-relative width and height, with the 3D camera itself adapting to whatever the real result is.
- **A genuine, honest fallback for fullscreen, not just a hopeful assumption**: `requestFullscreen()` is correctly called synchronously from a real click in both games (confirmed, not assumed), but browsers can still silently reject it for reasons outside this component's control — most notably, being embedded in an iframe without an explicit fullscreen permission on that iframe itself, which isn't something fixable from within this file. Both games now show a real "⛶ Fullscreen" button whenever fullscreen isn't currently active, giving the browser a fresh, genuine user-gesture-triggered chance to grant it.
- **Verified with the specific discipline this exact file's history demands**: a full re-scan of Wrath of Olympus's `render()` function for the bare-undeclared-variable bug class this file has a real production crash history with — zero found.

- **Fullscreen was already there too, just not wired to resize anything** — `requestFullscreen()` already existed in `begin()`, targeting `document.documentElement` directly. Added the same real state tracking as Wrath of Olympus, confirming fullscreen actually succeeded before the game area resizes to match.
- **HUD and controls genuinely moved inside the fullscreen container**: the resource bar, the milestone and caravan-offer popups, notices, story flash, the build menu, and the instructions line are all real children of the same container that expands to fill the screen.

Rather than rush a new mousemove-based 3D raycasting feature into an already-large change, that capability was left out of this pass on purpose. The tooltip's own display code is untouched and ready; it just won't currently be triggered. A real, scoped follow-up, clearly named rather than quietly lost.
- **Dead code cleaned up the same way as Wrath of Olympus**: `handleCanvasClick` and `handleCanvasMouseMove` both became genuinely unused once the 2D canvas lost its handlers — removed both, and cleaned up the resulting stale comments (including one still referencing "3D preview" terminology that no longer applies) rather than leaving a confusing trail.
- **`KingdomsOfAsh3D.js`'s wrapper now fills 100% of its parent** instead of a fixed 320px height, matching the exact fix already applied to `WrathScene3D.js` — the parent container controls real sizing now, whether that's a small panel or the full fullscreen viewport.
- **Full standard verification**: ref-integrity check (41 declared, 41 genuinely used), a setter-integrity check fully clean (the two flagged items reconfirmed as the same known false positives from earlier this session), both syntax checkers on every edit across all three files, and a full project-wide consistency check.
- **Both games now share the same real architecture**: static world, live units, real integration, real interaction, and now 3D-only fullscreen — the complete staged plan, applied consistently to both.
- **Wrath of Olympus — 3D is now the real, only, primary map, fullscreen, with HUD and controls overlaid on it.** Not a preview anymore. The 2D canvas is hidden (not removed — it still silently drives all game logic, since the entire tick loop lives in the same function that draws to it) and the 3D view is the actual visual, filling the screen in fullscreen mode.
- **Fullscreen was already half-built and undiscovered** — `requestFullscreen()` already existed in `startGame()`, matching a pattern from other games in this codebase, but nothing tracked whether it actually succeeded or resized the game area to match. Added real state tracking via the browser's own `fullscreenchange` event, so the game only resizes once fullscreen is confirmed active, not assumed the instant it's requested.
- **HUD and controls genuinely moved inside the fullscreen container**, not just visually similar: the resource bar, Hydra boss bar, notices, story flash, both choice modals, the power buttons, and the instructions line are all real children of the same container that expands to fill the screen — matching "all game info including controls and HUD should be within that fullscreen" directly.

This included catching a real, easy-to-miss gap: the champion/enemy/ward render loop still used a hardcoded Y=0 for live units, which would have made every moving unit float above or sink into the new heightmap terrain. Found and fixed before shipping, with all three now sampling the same real height function the ground itself is built from.

Caught by directly comparing the real file against the sandbox values, not assumed complete because the torches alone were touched.

Built a standalone test and confirmed the raycast hit's height exactly matches what `terrainHeight()` predicts at that same position, and swept the full horizontal screen range to map out exactly where hits succeed — confirming the entire real playable map (0–800 in game coordinates) falls well inside the region that correctly registers clicks, with only genuine off-map background clicks safely returning no hit.
- **Full standard verification**: ref-integrity check (41 declared, 41 genuinely used), a setter-integrity check fully clean (the two flagged items reconfirmed as the same known false positives from earlier this session), both syntax checkers on every edit across both files, a full project-wide consistency check, and direct confirmation that the click-handling wiring is correct at every point — the 2D canvas's original path still calls the same extracted function, and the 3D view's `onWorldClick` prop is correctly connected end to end.

- **Kingdoms of Ash — real 3D, genuinely integrated into the live game file.** A new `KingdomsOfAsh3D.js` component renders the real terrain (using either map the game actually supports, read live from `mapRef`), the British castle redesign, and every real building, villager, and bandit — reading directly from this game's own actual `buildingsRef`, `villagersRef`, and `banditsRef`, not a separate demo with fake data. Added as a "3D Preview (Beta)" panel below the real game canvas; the existing canvas still handles 100% of actual gameplay and input, completely untouched.
- **A real, serious bug caught and fixed before it ever reached the integration** — not after: an early version of the live-sync logic rebuilt each villager and bandit's entire figure from scratch every single frame to reposition it, using `Group.clear()`, which removes children but never disposes their geometry or materials. Since these units move constantly, that would have silently leaked new geometry every frame for every unit, forever, and also recreated ~13 mesh objects per unit every frame for no reason. Caught by actually reasoning through what happens over multiple frames, not just checking that one frame render succeeded.
- **The fix verified directly, not just reasoned about**: built an isolated test simulating a live entity moving across three separate frames, and confirmed the same Group object is reused, its mesh count stays exactly stable (13 across all three frames), and its position genuinely updates to match new data — the specific behavior the bug would have broken, checked with real numbers.
- **Every color is derived, not duplicated** — `BUILDING_COLORS_HEX` is computed directly from `BUILDING_TYPES`'s own real color strings (matching the same pattern already used for Wrath of Olympus's enemy colors), so the 3D view can never silently drift out of sync with the actual game's colors.
- **Reads the real active map, not a hardcoded one** — this game supports two different maps (Verdant Vale and Emberfall Coast), and the integration reads terrain, water, forests, and the town center position live from `mapRef.current` rather than assuming which map is in play.
- **Full standard verification**: ref-integrity check (41 declared, 41 genuinely used), a setter-integrity check fully clean (the two flagged items reconfirmed as the same known false positives from earlier this session), both syntax checkers on every edit across both files, a full project-wide consistency check, and direct confirmation that every prop name at the integration call site matches the component's actual parameters character-for-character.

- **Zero targeting logic was duplicated to make this work.** The exact function the real 2D canvas has always used (`handleWorldClick`) was extracted from the click handler it lived inside, with the 2D canvas's own behavior completely unchanged — it now just computes coordinates and calls that same function. The 3D view calls the identical function, just supplying coordinates computed a different way (a raycast against the ground plane instead of 2D screen math). Ward placement, power casting, and champion attack/move targeting all run through the one real, already-verified code path regardless of which view was clicked.
- **The raycasting math was actually tested, not just reviewed.** Built a standalone harness replicating the exact camera and ground setup, then simulated real clicks at multiple screen positions and checked the results against what the math should produce: a click at the exact center of the screen landed at (750, 500) — precisely the real map's center — and every directional test (up, down, left, right) moved the resulting coordinates in the mathematically correct direction. A click aimed above the ground plane's horizon correctly returned no hit at all, and the real production code already handles that case safely by simply not firing the callback.

- **A serious, confirmed platform incompatibility found and avoided before it could break production**: React Three Fiber's custom React reconciler was directly tested against this project's exact Next.js 15 + React 18.3.1 versions using the real `next build` command this project's own deploy pipeline runs — and it reproducibly failed the build outright (`TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'ReactCurrentBatchConfig')`), even after testing two genuine workarounds (forcing dynamic rendering, externalizing the package from the server bundle) that both still failed. This wasn't found from a blog post — it was reproduced firsthand, twice, before being ruled out.
- **The real fix verified working, not assumed**: raw Three.js via `useEffect` and a mount ref — the exact same pattern this codebase already trusts in four other games (`TitanArena.js`, `Dominion.js`, `ApexCircuit.js`, `GrandPrixDuel.js`) — was tested against the same real build command and succeeded cleanly. `three` was already a dependency at the right version; no `@react-three/fiber` was ever added.
- **The specific import path this component needed was verified too, not just the base package**: the postprocessing addons (`EffectComposer`, `RenderPass`, `UnrealBloomPass`, `OutputPass`) use a different import path than plain `three`, and that path was separately tested against a real production build on this project's exact pinned Three.js version (`^0.160.0`) before being trusted.
- **Live data, not a static scene**: champions, enemies, and Wards are tracked in per-entity Maps keyed by their own real ids, created once when an entity first appears and disposed the moment it's gone — the same entities the real 2D game is already tracking, mirrored in 3D every frame.
- **Enemy colors are derived, not duplicated** — `ENEMY_COLORS_HEX` is computed directly from `ENEMY_TYPES`'s own real color strings, so the 3D view can never silently drift out of sync with the actual game's colors.
- **Verified with the specific discipline this exact file's history demands**: a full re-scan of the entire `render()` function for the bare-undeclared-variable bug class this file has a real production crash history with — zero found — plus every prop name at the integration call site checked character-for-character against the component's actual parameters.

What's real and shipped: proper cast shadows and consistent shading across this game's most prominent structures.
- **Both the Temple and the Enemy Altar now have real cast shadows** — a soft radial gradient fading to transparent, the same proven technique from Kingdoms of Ash's buildings — giving both structures genuine ground contact instead of floating flat on the terrain. Checked directly first: the temple already had real gradient shading on its fluted columns, but neither structure had a shadow at all.
- **The Altar's pillars now have real gradient shading** matching the temple's columns — found and fixed a genuine inconsistency where the temple looked more finished than the structure it's meant to visually mirror, since the altar's pillars were still flat, single-color fills.
- **Guardians rebuilt with a real faceted hexagon shape and their own cast shadow**, replacing a plain filled circle — the same "real shape over flat circle" standard already applied to this game's enemy types, extended to cover the one unit type that was still a step behind.
- **Verified with the specific discipline this exact file's history demands**: a full re-scan of the entire `render()` function for the bare-undeclared-variable bug class this file has a real production crash history with — zero found.

- **Roads and stone quarries genuinely reworked, with the same honest ceiling as before — real, stylized 2D detail, not photorealism.** Roads now have real layered depth: a darker rutted base beneath a lighter dirt-toned surface, plus scattered pebble/wear marks along the actual path curve, instead of one flat brown stroke.
- **The pebbles are placed with the real quadratic Bezier formula**, not approximated — verified mathematically (the sampled points match Canvas's own curve definition exactly at both endpoints and along the curve), so they land precisely on the road's curved sections instead of drifting off to the side.
- **Stone quarries now show a real dug-out pit** (a radial gradient darkening toward the center, suggesting actual depth) with individually shaded rock chunks — each with its own lit/shaded gradient and a real cast shadow — instead of flat single-color polygons sitting on a flat grey rectangle.
- **Verified with full discipline**: syntax checks on every edit, ref-integrity and setter-integrity checks (both clean, the two flagged items reconfirmed as the same known false positives from earlier this session), a full project-wide consistency check, and the rock chunk positions checked against the quarry's actual 30×30 footprint to confirm nothing renders outside its bounds.
- **The Town Center is now genuinely multi-story** — three stacked, tapering tiers (each narrower than the one below), each with its own gradient shading and window, capped with the roof and spire. This is a real, standard way 2D games suggest height and structure without true 3D geometry — the shapes are still flat, but the silhouette itself now reads as an actual tower.
- **Verified with real numbers, not eyeballed — and my first version was wrong**: a first pass compressed all three tiers into the original building's existing footprint height, and running the actual math showed it came out barely 1.0x the old single-story silhouette — not a multi-story building at all, just the same height subdivided into bands. Redesigned with tiers that genuinely extend past the original footprint, re-verified at 1.34x the previous total height, and then re-extracted the exact multipliers straight out of the shipped file to confirm the real code matches what was simulated, not just trusted from the design pass.
- **Verified with full discipline**: syntax checks on every edit, ref-integrity and setter-integrity checks (both clean, the two flagged items reconfirmed as the same known false positives from earlier this session), and a full project-wide consistency check.
- **Kingdoms of Ash — a genuine visual upgrade started, with an honest ceiling stated upfront.** A real 3D-rendered RTS look (the reference image shared) isn't achievable here — this game runs on Canvas 2D, not a 3D graphics pipeline, and there's no 3D modeling tooling available to build or preview real geometry, lighting, or shadow-mapped terrain. Rather than promise something that can't be delivered, this is a real, meaningfully better version of what Canvas 2D actually supports: shading, real depth cues, and more detailed silhouettes.
- **Every building now gets a genuine soft drop shadow** — a radial gradient fading to transparent, replacing the previous flat, thin dark bar every building had. This alone gives every structure on the map a real sense of standing above the ground rather than being painted flat onto it.
- **The House and Town Center rebuilt with real shading and detail** — gradient-shaded walls (a lit side and a shaded side, suggesting an actual light source instead of one flat fill color), a genuine two-tone roof, and, new on the House specifically, a real framed window with cross-mullions and window glass tint, plus a proper door with its own frame instead of a bare colored rectangle standing in for one.
- **Verified with full discipline**: syntax checks on every edit, ref-integrity and setter-integrity checks (both clean, the two flagged items reconfirmed as the same known false positives from earlier this session), and a full project-wide consistency check.

- **Wrath of Olympus — a real boss fight at wave 14, the fourth and final piece of the "10x more interesting" evolution.** The Hydra replaces the standard crowd wave with a single, distinct encounter: it regenerates a real chunk of missing HP if left unpressured for 4+ seconds (mythologically accurate — growing new heads — and mechanically the reason a slow chip-damage strategy shouldn't work against it), and it spits venom at range, threatening champions and wards even when they're not in melee with it.
- **Damage detection built the safe way, not the risky one**: rather than instrumenting every damage source in this large file (champion melee, ward attacks, eight different god powers) to explicitly stamp a "last hit" timestamp — real risk of missing one and silently breaking the regen condition — the Hydra's regen is driven by comparing its own HP tick-to-tick. One central check, not eight scattered ones.
- **Lives in the exact same enemy array every other enemy does**, deliberately, so it correctly participates in the wave-clear check, champion/ward interception, and the generic kill-detection logic with zero special-casing needed for any of that — only its unique mechanics (regen, venom, and grinding the temple down instead of a one-hit kamikaze death like an ordinary enemy) needed new code at all.
- **A genuinely distinct three-headed silhouette**, not a scaled-up reuse of any existing enemy shape, with its own on-battlefield HP bar and a second, prominent boss bar in the HUD — the classic "this is the fight that matters right now" signal.
- **Balance verified with real numbers, not assumed — and my first instinct was actually wrong**: simulated three build scenarios (a realistic spread of upgrades, an extreme min-maxed build, and an undergeared one with no champion upgrades at all) before settling on the boss's HP. My first reaction to a too-fast 5.5-second kill time was to just raise the HP — but simulating the *realistic* build first showed a 14-second fight, and the undergeared case nearly 20 (likely longer in real play, given movement and positioning the simulation doesn't account for). The original number was already right; raising it would have overcorrected for an edge case, not fixed a real problem.
- **A real safety check specific to this exact game's history**: a new enemy type existing at all meant every place that generically reads `ENEMY_TYPES[en.typeId]` needed a real entry for it (name, damage, radius, color, faithReward) — added deliberately, not discovered as a crash later, since skipping it would have meant `undefined.damage` the instant a Hydra spawned.
- **Verified with the full discipline this file's history demands, one more time**: syntax and balance checks on every edit, a full re-scan of the entire `render()` function for the bare-undeclared-variable bug class this file has a real production crash history with — zero found — a setter-integrity check, and a full project-wide consistency check.

- **Wrath of Olympus — placeable defenses (Wards), the third of four pieces toward making this game genuinely 10x more interesting.** Click "Place Ward," then click the battlefield — up to 3 stationary defenses (50 Faith each, a real opportunity cost against spending that same Faith on God Powers), each intercepting and fighting nearby enemies the same way a champion does, so where you place one genuinely changes how a wave plays out, not just how much total damage you have.
- **A real, separate, more consequential bug found and fixed while building this — not a footnote**: champions have had `hp`/`maxHp` fields and a rendered HP bar since the direct-control work earlier, but nothing anywhere ever reduced a champion's HP from enemy contact. They were functionally invulnerable despite the UI implying otherwise. Building Wards on top of that same gap would have made them invulnerable too — a genuinely bad foundation for a mechanic whose whole point is real stakes. Fixed properly: enemies now deal real periodic damage to champions and Wards in contact range, and Wards can be destroyed if left unsupported.
- **A real respawn system, not just "champions can now die"**: an 8-second respawn with a visible on-screen countdown, so losing your player champion costs real tempo without permanently locking you out of the control this whole effort has been building toward. A death-moment flash and a return-to-the-fight flash both fire at the actual right moments, not just on a timer.
- **The UI is genuinely wired up, not just the game logic**: a real "Place Ward" button matching the exact look and interaction pattern of a God Power button (arm it, then click the battlefield), full mutual exclusion in both directions (arming a power cancels an in-progress ward placement and vice versa — checked and fixed in both `handlePowerButtonClick` and the new button, not just one), and the ward count is synced into the same `hud` state object every other ref-backed UI value already uses, so the button's disabled state stays live rather than reading a ref directly during render.
- **Faith re-validated at the actual moment of placement, not just when the button was pressed** — since a power could be cast in between, spending the Faith the placement needs.
- **Verified with the specific discipline this exact file's history demands, one more time**: re-ran the full bare-undeclared-variable re-scan across the entire `render()` function — zero found — and specifically checked the one new `textAlign`-setting line added this round against every other `fillText` call in the file (both of the only two that exist already set their own alignment immediately before use, so there was no leak risk to introduce).

- **One piece left from what was requested**: real boss fights.
- **Wrath of Olympus — a real mid-run upgrade system, the second of four pieces toward making this game genuinely 10x more interesting.** After every wave, a real choice: 3 randomly-offered upgrades to your player champion (more HP with a full heal, more damage, more speed, faster attacks, or more range), stacking across the whole run. A wave-13 champion who's been built up should feel dramatically stronger than a fresh wave-1 one — verified with real numbers before shipping, not just assumed: 13 picks of Might brings damage from 18 to 83 (~4.6x), 13 picks of Vitality brings max HP from 120 to 445 (~3.7x) — meaningful growth, not a rounding error, but not so extreme it trivializes the escalating enemy scaling already in place.
- **A real, caught-before-shipping balance bug**: Fury Edge reduces attack cooldown by 80ms per pick — stacked enough times across a 13-wave run, that would push the computed attack rate to zero or negative, breaking the cooldown check entirely rather than just capping out. Caught by actually running the math, not just eyeballing the numbers, and fixed with an explicit 200ms floor.
- **Reuses this game's own existing "pause and offer a choice" pattern exactly** — the same modal shape the rare god-tier unlocks already use, so the player learns one interaction pattern, not two differently-styled UIs.
- **The next wave now waits on the player's actual pick instead of starting on a fixed timer** — a real decision each time, not a countdown to sit through.
- **Upgrades are correctly per-run**: `championUpgradesRef` resets to zero in the same place every other per-run reference already resets, confirmed by checking the actual reset function line by line rather than assuming a new ref would be caught by an existing pattern automatically.
