A mouse-aimed arc attack with an actual 3-hit combo chain (each hit within the timing window builds toward the next, resets if you're too slow), a dash with real invulnerability frames, and a heavier cooldown-based special — the specific feel Hades is known for. Wrapped in a deliberately different visual language than the rest of the tier: warm painterly tones, soft sun shafts, drifting light motes, ground fog, all inspired by Journey/Gris/Sky rather than the neon-dark palette used elsewhere. 3 rooms of enemies, a blessing choice between each (pick 1 of 2 permanent run upgrades), then a two-phase boss with a telegraphed area burst. The content budget in the spec was small and honored directly: 1 biome, 1 weapon, 3 enemies, 1 boss.
- **Kingdoms of Ash** — a ninth Legend Pass game, and the pace change of the whole tier: a calm kingdom builder, no reflexes involved. Pick a building from the palette, click the grass to place it, and villagers walk to work entirely on their own — gathering wood, farming food, quarrying stone, minting gold — then bring it back and go again. A full day-night cycle runs with buildings glowing warm at night, the season visibly drifts from spring green toward autumn amber over the 5-minute session, rain passes through occasionally, and bandits periodically test your Watch Towers for real (light) stakes. Built from a spec asking for 1000 villagers and a massive procedural map — scoped down to one hand-placed map and 7 building types, keeping the actual "living world you want to watch" feeling intact. No keyboard input at all, so there's nothing here that could ever hit the arrow-key scroll bug.
- **Neon Drift** — an eighth Legend Pass game, built from a spec that was actually easy to honor faithfully: "reduce content, increase visual fidelity" (only 3 tracks, 5 vehicles, one city). A pseudo-3D perspective-projected road — the classic arcade-racer technique of drawing the track as shrinking trapezoid segments toward a horizon — giving real curves and a genuine sense of speed and depth without a 3D engine. Rain, occasional lightning flashes with real screen shake, a glowing procedural skyline, neon-lined road edges, speed streaks that radiate faster as you accelerate, boost flame particles, and 3 AI rivals actually racing for position (not just decorative). 5 selectable cars with real stat differences, 3 tracks with distinct curve patterns. Desktop-only, fullscreen from the start.
- **Operation Blacksite** — a seventh Legend Pass game, and another genuinely different genre: mouse-aimed hitscan shooting (not auto-attack, not swing-combat), a round-based buy-phase economy (earn money from kills and round wins, spend it on 3 weapons and armor between rounds), a bomb plant/defuse objective instead of a survival timer, and AI bots with real behavior states — patrol, alert/search, engage with actual return fire, and rush-the-bomb once it's planted. Built from a large Counter-Strike-inspired production spec; scoped down to one well-designed map and best-of-5 rounds, with real visual investment since that was explicitly requested: bullet tracers, muzzle flashes, a soft dynamic light around the player, screen shake and a damage flash on taking hits, and a low-health vignette. Headshots deal 2.5x damage. Desktop-only (mouse-aim genuinely needs it), fullscreen from the start.
- **Driftlight** — a sixth Legend Pass game, and the first one where combat is genuinely secondary rather than the point. Built from a spec asking for "the most beautiful browser game ever" (six biomes, wall-running, a grapple hook, five-layer adaptive orchestral music, photo mode) — scoped honestly down to one continuous world with real movement feel: acceleration/deceleration instead of instant velocity, coyote time, jump buffering, a double jump from the start, a glide, and a dash. A day-night sky cycle, three visual zones that blend into each other rather than hard-cutting, ~45 ambient drifting particles, and a player rendered as a soft glowing spirit (layered canvas glow, no sprite grid at all) rather than the pixel-block style used everywhere else. 12 collectible fragments, one spectacle boss encounter framed as restoration rather than combat.
- **The arrow-key page-scroll bug fixed everywhere, not just the games it was reported in** — audited every single game on the site for keyboard input (17 games total use arrow keys or WASD), and found the same missing `preventDefault()` in 9 more that hadn't been reported yet: Apex Circuit, Grand Prix Duel, Beat Rush, Block Cascade, Fruit Chase, Maze Muncher, Lane Dash, Peak Ascent, and Neon Snake. Every one of them now correctly blocks the browser's default scroll behavior. 3 games (Pixel Jumper, Platform Quest, Turbo Circuit) already had this handled correctly and needed no change.
- **Fixed a real bug affecting all 4 existing horde-survival games**: arrow keys were scrolling the whole page alongside character movement, because the keyboard handler never called `preventDefault()`. Fixed identically in Bloodmoon Siege, Starfall Overrun, Arcane Survivor, and Arena Survivor.
- **Shadowfall** — a fifth Legend Pass game, and a genuinely different genre this time, not a reskin: a real 2D platformer with actual gravity and jump physics, a camera that scrolls through a world wider than the screen, manual player-directed melee combat (not auto-attack), and ability-gated exploration — you find Double Jump on an early ledge, which is the only way to cross a chasm blocking the back half of the map, leading to a boss with a real two-phase fight. Built from another large production spec (a metroidvania inspired by Hollow Knight/Ori/Ender Lilies); scoped down from "vast interconnected kingdom" to one complete, honest vertical slice — real physics and progression over a sprawling but shallow world. Desktop-only, fullscreen from the start, with the arrow-key bug fixed from the very first line of its input code.
- **Arena Survivor** — a fourth Legend Pass game, built from another large production spec (Brotato × Vampire Survivors, React/Canvas-only). This spec's tech constraints actually matched the existing architecture well, so the focus here was picking the single biggest structural differentiator from the other 3 Legend Pass games: **discrete waves separated by a shop**, not continuous survival with random cards. 8 waves, a Material currency earned from kills, a real shop between each wave (buy weapons or stat items, choose what you can afford), a genuine weapon-merging system (3 of the same weapon at the same tier auto-fuse into a stronger one), a damage-on-death Exploder enemy, and two bosses with distinct mechanics — a mid-run Arena Guardian and a final Void Overlord, both with a telegraphed charge-dash attack. Desktop-only and fullscreen from the start.
- **Arcane Survivor** — a third horde-survival roguelite for Legend Pass, built from a genuinely large production spec (full weapon evolution trees, 5 characters, meta-progression, procedural open worlds, Supabase-backed leaderboards, Phaser 3/Vite/TypeScript as a separate stack). Rather than fragment the site into multiple deployables to chase every system in that spec, this delivers a real, focused subset on the same proven architecture as the other two: up to 3 simultaneous weapons (Arcane Bolt, Fire Wand, Ice Spear) each firing independently, a genuine weapon evolution (max Arcane Bolt + carry the Ancient Tome passive → Arcane Nova, a full radial burst instead of single-target), rarity-tiered upgrade cards with 4 real tiers, elite enemy variants every minute, and a boss with an actual phase transition (a telegraphed slam at half health, not just a bigger health bar). Desktop-only and fullscreen from the start.
- **All Legend Pass games now open in fullscreen and are gated to laptop/desktop screens** — Titan Arena already had this; Duskward and Bloodmoon Siege were retrofitted, and Starfall Overrun has it from the start. This is mentioned on the dashboard (Legend Pass section), on every Legend Pass game's own page, and as a "💻 Desktop only" badge on each game's card — nobody discovers this by hitting a wall on their phone.
- **Starfall Overrun** — a second horde-survival roguelite for Legend Pass, mechanically distinct from Bloodmoon Siege on purpose: no ranged auto-fire, instead an orbiting blade that continuously circles you (positioning-based combat) plus a periodic Pulse Nova burst. Original sci-fi theme and enemy roster, 9 upgrade choices, two Void Titan boss waves, same pixel-sprite and particle-effect treatment as Bloodmoon Siege.
- **Titan Arena: AI difficulty select** — Rookie/Veteran/Champion, chosen right after picking your fighter, scaling how aggressive and tactical the AI plays (reusing the personality-trait system) with a real score multiplier reward (up to ×2) for tougher opponents.
- **Bloodmoon Siege** — a new Legend Pass game, a horde-survival roguelite in the spirit of games like Vampire Survivors (an original theme and roster, not a reproduction). Auto-attacking combat, XP gems, a real level-up choice every level (7 distinct upgrades), enemies that ramp up over a 4-minute run, two Reaper boss waves, genuine pixel-grid sprites, and real particle effects on every hit/kill/level-up. Canvas-based for the performance headroom a horde needs — no new infrastructure, deploys the same as every other game.
- **Workers.dev address now redirects to tapandscore.com** — anyone who reaches the raw `workers.dev` URL gets sent to your real domain automatically.
- **Duskward genuinely deepened** — this was the honest goal: real combat decisions (Power Strike and Second Wind, both costing a new stamina resource, alongside plain Attack), 3 zones with distinct monster pools that unlock as you level (Whispering Woods → Bonevale Crypt → Shadowfen Marsh), 2 new tougher monster types for the later zones, and a proper quest log holding 2 quests at once instead of just 1. Same simple architecture, same one-file-upload deploy — just meaningfully more game underneath it.
- **Duskward** — a single-player dark fantasy RPG, and a genuinely different kind of game from everything else on the site: it has a persistent character (name, level, gold, gear, quests) that's saved every time you play, not just a one-off score. Explore the forest, fight Wolves/Skeletons/Spiders/Goblins, level up, find loot across 4 rarity tiers (common/rare/epic/legendary), equip weapons and armor, take on kill and collect quests from the quest giver, and face the Dungeon Boss once you hit level 5. Gated behind Legend Pass, built and deployed exactly like every other game — one file upload, one migration, no new infrastructure.
- **"Premium" and "Premium Plus" renamed to Power Pass and Legend Pass** — game-style branding instead of generic SaaS language, updated everywhere it shows up: dashboard categories, landing page, account page, checkout descriptions, day pass, referral copy, refund policy. Legend Pass price dropped from ₹199 to ₹149/month.
- **Titan Arena: combo-chaining** — consecutive hits landed within 1.8 seconds build a real stacking damage bonus (up to +42% at a 6-hit chain), not just a counter for show, with a visible on-screen combo indicator. Breaks if you get hit, or if the window lapses.
- **Titan Arena: personality-driven AI** — each of the 10 fighters now has real behavioral traits (aggression, how often it blocks, how eagerly it reaches for specials), so an AI opponent's playstyle genuinely reflects who they are — plus real tactical behavior: a character with a heal special now reaches for it specifically when hurt, not at random.
- **Titan Arena pushed further within its real ceiling** — procedural canvas-based armor texture (real surface detail instead of flat color, generated in-browser with zero external assets), soft glow halos behind emissive points (eyes, chest stripe) faking a bloom effect without the risk of a full post-processing pipeline, tapered cylinder limbs instead of boxes for a rounder silhouette, squash-and-stretch on every hit landed, a CSS-based cinematic vignette + color grade over the game view, and a real slow-motion "FINISH" freeze-frame beat on the match-deciding blow. All of this uses only core Three.js and standard browser APIs — zero new dependencies, zero deployment risk.
- **Titan Arena's battle music rebuilt with real intensity** — an original composition (not a reproduction of any existing game's actual soundtrack) built around a two-voice tribal drum pattern (a deep pitch-dropping kick + a sharp rim hit), a driving dissonant bassline, and sparse brass-stab accents for dramatic punctuation, at a faster 150 BPM. This is genre-inspired — dark, percussive, "the fight is starting" energy — the same principle applied everywhere else in this build: original composition capturing the feel, not a copy of the actual copyrighted score.
- **Titan Arena visual overhaul** — real shadows (shadow-mapped lighting, fighters cast and receive them), smoothly tweened animations instead of instant pose-snapping, more detailed character models (shoulder armor, gloves, boots, glowing eyes, a swaying cape), hit-impact particle sparks, and camera shake when you take damage. Important honest note: this is a genuine, substantial upgrade within what a browser-based Three.js game can achieve — it will not and cannot look like a photorealistic console game (that requires a completely different pipeline: professional 3D art, motion capture, a dedicated game engine — not something achievable through generated code in a browser).
- **Racing controls eased significantly further** — the track is now over 50% wider (7.4 → 11.5 units), steering is noticeably more responsive, off-track and corner penalties are softer, and the random "loss of control" wobble on hard corners has been removed entirely.
- **Real visual depth added to both racing games** — a distant ring of hills gives the horizon actual depth, a start/finish arch marks the lap line, and the car model itself got mirrors, exhaust pipes, bright wheel rims, and a subtle colored underglow. All of this lives in the shared track-building library, so both Apex Circuit and Grand Prix Duel got it from one set of changes.
- **2 genuinely original free games**: Echo Chase (a live replay of your own past movement becomes a hazard chasing you — not a reskin of any existing genre) and Pulse Maze (cross a field of rhythm-timed gates, a mechanic distinct from the dodge-based Lane Dash). Both have the same auto-continuing level progression as the rest of the free tier.
- **Fixed a real schema gap**: the `subscriptions` table only ever allowed one subscription per user (single-column primary key), which is exactly why Premium Plus couldn't show up as an independent option on the account page — there was no room for a second one. Changed to a composite key `(user_id, plan_id)` so a user can hold Premium and Premium Plus completely independently, and updated every route that touches this table (`hasSubscriptionAccess`, cancel-subscription, subscribe) to be plan-aware.
- **Account page rewritten to be tier-aware** — it now shows a separate card for every subscription tier that has games behind it (Premium, Premium Plus, and any future tier automatically), each with its own status, subscribe button, and cancel flow.
- **Landing page game ordering now matches the dashboard** — same Arcade-first, Premium-Plus-second, Premium-third ordering in both places, so what a visitor sees before signing up matches what they see after.
- **Removed the "100% FREE" corner badge** from the landing page — back to the plain button, per feedback.

## Deployment resilience — how this actually works

**The good news first:** Cloudflare Workers deployments are already atomic. When you run `wrangler deploy`, there is no window where the site is half-updated or down — traffic switches to the new version instantly at the edge. Existing users don't get disrupted by the deploy itself.

**The one real edge case, on any Next.js host:** if someone's page loaded *before* a deploy, and they then click a link to a part of the site they haven't visited yet, their browser tries to fetch a JS chunk that the new deploy has since replaced. Without handling this, it shows up as a hard crash. **`components/DeploymentGuard.js`** (loaded site-wide from the root layout) now catches exactly this failure and shows a calm, dismissible "a newer version is ready — refresh whenever you like" prompt instead — and critically, it never auto-refreshes. Someone mid-game keeps playing on the old code until they choose to refresh. Nothing is ever pulled out from under them.

**For updating one specific game without disrupting the rest of the site:** a new `under_maintenance` flag on the `games` table (separate from `is_active`, which fully removes a game). Set it to `true` before pushing a risky change to one game, and instead of a broken page, players see a calm "🔧 Game engine updating" message — both on that game's page and as a small badge on its dashboard card, so nobody clicks in blind. Flip it back to `false` the moment the new version is confirmed working. Every other game and the rest of the site are completely unaffected the whole time.

**Scaling note:** both Cloudflare Workers and Supabase are already serverless/auto-scaling — there's no fixed server count to manage as traffic grows. The levers that actually matter at higher scale are your Cloudflare Workers plan tier (request limits) and Supabase's plan tier (database connections/compute), not this codebase's architecture.
- **Titan Arena expanded to a full 10-fighter roster**, each with 2 genuinely distinct special abilities (9 different mechanical effects across the roster — ranged, unblockable, guard-break, stun, heal, dash, knockback, poison damage-over-time, lifesteal — not just re-skinned damage numbers)
- **Dedicated battle music** — a separate, more intense track from the site's general arcade music, starting the moment the arena opens (character select), through the whole fight
- **Every fighter has its own combat sound** — punches, kicks, and specials are all built from that character's own sound voice (frequency, waveform, glide direction), so all 10 fighters sound audibly different in a fight, not shared generic sfx
- **New "Premium Plus" tier** (₹199/month) — a separate subscription above Premium, not bundled in. Existing Premium subscribers do NOT get access automatically.
- **Titan Arena** — the Premium Plus flagship: a genuinely full-3D fighting game (real 3D character models built and rigged in Three.js, not the 2D-abstracted combat used elsewhere in the site). Fullscreen on entry, laptop/desktop only (shows a clear message and blocks play on touch-primary or small screens). First-wave roster of 3 original fighters — Kael Ashborn, Nyx Frostwind, Raiju Volt — each with a genuinely distinct special move (ranged, guard-break, and unblockable respectively), not just re-skinned damage numbers. This is an original game, not a reproduction of any existing commercial title or its characters.
- **Every game in the catalog now has difficulty/level progression — 100% coverage.** This batch finished the remaining strategy and racing titles: Colony Rush (building milestones), Empire Command & Dominion (6-level AI difficulty — Dominion's change was deliberately limited to the AI's numeric parameters only, not touching any of its WebGL rendering or click-handling code), Turbo Circuit (distance milestones with a genuinely narrowing timing window), Territory Duel (6-level AI difficulty for its AI-fallback mode, multiplayer matches unaffected), and Apex Circuit (each completed lap is now a real level-up moment with a bonus, not just a counter)
- **5 more free games get level progression** (20 of 21 free games now leveled): Reflex Tap (a real difficulty ladder — the reaction window shrinks each level, run ends when you're too slow), Strike Zone (milestone-based, more targets + faster spawns), Platform Quest (distance milestones), Tic Tac Duel (rewritten as an endless AI ladder — beat the computer and a smarter one steps up, draws just replay, only a loss ends the run), Creature Clash (the 5-battle gauntlet is now endless — every win brings a tougher opponent forever, with "Tier" celebrations every 5 battles)
- **6 more free games get level progression** (15 total now): Neon Snake, Mole Rush, Typing Rush, Color Rush (all milestone-based: hit a target, level up, earn a real reward — bonus time or faster pace), Lane Dash and Pixel Jumper (distance milestones with bonus points)
- **Fixed a real, serious bug in Pixel Jumper**: it was submitting a score of 0 every single run, regardless of actual distance traveled, due to a stale closure capturing the score at game-start instead of game-end. Found and fixed while adding leveling — also fixed the same category of bug in Lane Dash, where the obstacle speed never actually increased in gameplay even though the displayed number did.
- **7 more premium games get real level progression** (13 total now): Star Defender & Shell Squad (explicit wave celebrations added to existing scaling), Peak Ascent (100m milestones), Swarm Breach (worm speed + count scale with level), Fruit Chase (auto-continuing maze, same pattern as Maze Muncher), Horizon Guardian (rescue milestones + scaling abductor spawns), and Iron Fist (a real opponent ladder — beat one rival and a tougher one steps up immediately, score carries, until you actually lose a match)
- **Richer sound across every single game, free or premium** — upgraded the shared sound engine itself (smoother attack envelopes, a layered "chorus" harmony on positive sounds) rather than touching each game file individually, so all ~38 games benefit automatically. Added a dedicated "level up" fanfare, distinct from the personal-best sound.
- **Level progression extended to 6 free games** (now 9 total with Maze Muncher/Brick Blaster/Block Cascade from before): Memory Match (bigger boards each level, 90-second session), Sequence, Digit Span, Word Scramble, Quick Trivia — each with real level-up feedback (sound + flash + haptic) and score that carries forward
- **6 premium games now have 6 difficulty levels instead of 4** (Sky Raiders, Void Drifter, Duel Arena, Rim Rockers, Beat Rush, and Grand Prix Duel's AI mode) — a real, distinct difficulty curve from Cadet through Legend, each with its own score multiplier
- **Auto-continuing level progression on free games** (Maze Muncher, Brick Blaster, Block Cascade) — clearing a maze/board doesn't end the run, it escalates (more critters, faster ball, faster drop) and keeps going with your score carried forward, until you actually lose. A "LEVEL X!" flash + sound + haptic pulse marks each level-up — designed to hook casual visitors into "just one more level" and give them a real taste of depth before they ever consider Premium. More free games will get the same treatment in a follow-up round.
- **No more splash gate** — the "PRESS START" moment is folded directly into the landing page hero (a small blinking prompt above the main button) instead of a separate blocking screen, matching the "no nonsense, no bloatware" positioning
- **Haptic feedback** on mobile — a light tap vibration on bottom-tab navigation, and a distinct celebratory pulse when you hit a new best or take #1 on a leaderboard (silently does nothing on devices without vibration support, like iOS Safari)
- **Instant-feeling page transitions** — dashboard, leaderboard, Hall of Fame, account, and every game page now show a branded loading screen the moment you tap, instead of the browser just sitting on the old page while data loads
- Removed accidental text-selection highlighting on rapid taps (the biggest remaining "this feels like a webpage, not an app" tell during fast game controls)
- **Easier, more forgiving racing controls** — steering is more responsive, the off-track and sharp-corner speed penalties are less punishing, and the random "loss of control" wobble on corners is much smaller — the track still requires real driving skill, but no longer fights the player.
- **AI difficulty levels 1–4** in Grand Prix Duel's "Race vs AI" mode (Rookie/Amateur/Pro/Legend) — each level genuinely changes the bot's top speed, cornering skill, and reaction smoothness, with a real score multiplier (×1 / ×1.3 / ×1.6 / ×2) for beating a tougher opponent
- **"PRESS START" splash screen** on the landing page — a real arcade-cabinet attract-mode gate. Tapping or pressing any key to enter is also the exact gesture browsers require before audio can play, so the soundtrack genuinely starts the instant someone enters the site (true zero-interaction autoplay isn't possible on any website — this is the closest real equivalent, and it's on-theme rather than a hidden technical requirement)
- **Richer, louder background music** — a 4-section evolving chiptune arrangement (not one repeating loop) with a percussive layer, volume more than tripled from before. The toggle button also now correctly shows sound is on by default and invites the first tap ("Tap for music") instead of misleadingly showing muted.
- **Difficulty levels (1/2/3)** on Sky Raiders, Void Drifter, Duel Arena, Rim Rockers, and Beat Rush — each level meaningfully changes the game's actual difficulty parameters (speed, spawn rate, AI toughness, timing windows), and higher levels award a real score multiplier (×1 / ×1.35 / ×1.8) for playing harder
- **Retro arcade background music** on the landing page and dashboard — a looping chiptune bassline + arpeggiated lead, synthesized on the fly (no audio files). A floating 🔊/🔇 toggle sits in the bottom corner of both pages; it's the same mute setting used everywhere else on the site, so turning it off there also silences in-game sound effects, and vice versa.
- **Real racing physics** — the car no longer auto-follows the track. Steering rotates the car's own heading; not turning through a corner drives you straight off it, exactly like a real car. Trees and gravel run-off now line the track too.
- **13 classic-arcade-inspired games** (all Premium): Star Defender, Void Drifter, Swarm Breach, Sky Raiders, Peak Ascent, Horizon Guardian, Duel Arena, Frontline Marksman, Fruit Chase, Iron Fist, Shell Squad, Rim Rockers, Beat Rush — original games inspired by genre classics, spanning shooters, a real 1v1 fighter, a maze chase with power-ups, a brawler, arcade basketball, and a rhythm game
- **Dashboard reordered** — Arcade category shown first, "Most Popular" card grid replaced with a "Most Played" ranked leaderboard list
- **Full account management** (`/account`) — subscription status with cancel, a ₹10/24-hour Premium day pass, complete one-time purchase history, and referral status, all in one place
- **Private feedback form** (`/feedback`) — goes straight to an admin-only panel, never shown to other users or published anywhere
- **Dominion background music** — a soft ambient pad, separate from sound effects, respecting the mute toggle
- **Dominion** — a genuine 3D strategy game (Premium): a real 19-territory hex map, WebGL rendering, tap-to-select/attack via raycasting, resource tiles that create a real "expand vs. develop" strategic choice
- **Territory Duel** — real 1v1 multiplayer (Premium), no AI: matchmaking finds you a live opponent, moves sync instantly via Supabase Realtime, and every move is validated server-side so neither player can cheat by tampering with their own client. Realtime is enabled for the `duels` table automatically by the migration — if live updates ever don't seem to arrive, double-check Supabase → Database → Replication shows `duels` as enabled.
- **Claim win on an inactive opponent** — if your Territory Duel opponent goes quiet mid-match, a "claim win" option appears after 60 seconds of silence on their turn, so no one's stuck waiting forever. Waiting-for-opponent searches can also be cancelled outright.
- **Grand Prix Duel** — live 1v1 multiplayer racing (Premium): a real opponent's car races alongside yours in real time, positions synced ~7x/second over Supabase Realtime's ephemeral Broadcast channels (no database writes for the live race itself, which is what keeps it fast)
- **Apex Circuit** — a genuine 3D racing game (Premium), built with real WebGL via Three.js: a 3D track, a chase camera, steering, and boost pads — not a 2D game with a 3D-sounding name
- **All game IP explicitly owned by Tap & Score** — stated in the site footer
- **Premium tier now has real depth** — the 4 most system-rich games (Colony Rush, Block Cascade, Platform Quest, Creature Clash) moved to the ₹100/month subscription, alongside Empire Command and Turbo Circuit — 6 deep games behind the paywall, not 2
- **👑 Premium badge** next to subscribers' names on every leaderboard — visible status, not just game access
- **Upgrade nudge** shown on free games to non-subscribers (hidden entirely for subscribers — this is what "ad-free" means here, rather than wiring up a third-party ad network)
- **Share my rank** button on the leaderboard page — one tap opens the native share sheet (or copies to clipboard) with a ready-made brag message and link back to the site
- **Installable on phones** — visitors can "Add to Home Screen" (Android/iOS) and it opens full-screen like a real app, with its own icon, no browser address bar
- A bottom tab bar on mobile (Games / Ranks / Fame / Account) — the desktop version still uses the top nav bar
- Email/password accounts (Supabase Auth), with self-serve **forgot password / reset password**
- **Admin accounts** — play every game free, and manage users (add, delete, reset password) from `/admin`. The first admin is set directly in Supabase (see Step 2 above); admins can't be created through the app itself, on purpose
- A public landing page at `tapandscore.com` — visitors see a marketing page and must sign up or log in before touching any game, leaderboard, or score
- 23 games across Reflex, Puzzle, Word, Strategy, Arcade, Shooter, and Premium categories — including 2 Premium games (Empire Command, an original strategy game; Turbo Circuit, an original racing game) behind a monthly subscription, and 5 flagship Arcade games (Platform Quest, Creature Clash, Brick Blaster, Block Cascade, Maze Muncher — all original, none of them clones)
- A dedicated **Leaderboard** page (`/leaderboard`) with Top 10, 11th–50th, and 51st+ shown as separate tiers, filterable by game or overall, with country flags next to every name
- A **Hall of Fame** (`/hall-of-fame`) for players who've earned 1,000,000,000+ lifetime points across every game they've ever played — a genuine career milestone, not a single good round
- A **celebration** (confetti + fanfare) when a score submission pushes a player to #1 in that game or #1 overall
- **Account settings** (`/account`) so any user can set or change their username and country (shown on leaderboards) after signing up
- **Retro sound effects on every game** — synthesized on the fly (no audio files to host), with a persistent mute toggle that remembers your choice
- An **Exit** option always available mid-game, with a confirmation step so it can't be tapped by accident
- A **Most Popular** section on the dashboard, ranked by real player counts
- Personal best score tracking per game
- Public leaderboards, per-game and overall
- Three ways a game can be priced — free, a one-time purchase, or part of a monthly subscription tier — all editable from Supabase's Table Editor, no code involved
- A real paywall: Fungies' webhook signature is verified on the
  server, subscription records are only ever written by server code
  with a service-role key, and the webhook keeps subscription status
  accurate automatically as Fungies charges (or fails to charge) each
  renewal
- Basic SEO: page titles, meta description, OpenGraph/Twitter tags, JSON-LD structured data, `robots.txt`, and `sitemap.xml`

## Editing prices and games later (no code, no GitHub)

Two tables in Supabase's **Table Editor** control everything about
what's on the site and what it costs:

- **`games`** — one row per game. Edit `name`, `icon`, `tagline`, or `category` to change how it's presented. Set `access_type` to `free`, `onetime`, or `subscription`. For a one-time game, edit `price_paise` (in paise — ₹125 is 12500) and `price_display` (₹125). For a subscription game, set `subscription_plan_id` to which tier unlocks it. Set `is_active` to `false` to hide a game without deleting it.

Every edit takes effect immediately — no redeploy needed.

## Adding a brand new game later

Config lives in the database, but gameplay is still real code — a new
game needs one small file:

1. Add a row to the `games` table in Supabase (slug, name, icon, category, accent color, tagline, `component_key`, and pricing as above).
2. Create a component in `games/YourGame.js`. It receives exactly two props — `onFinish(score)` (call it once when the game ends) and `accentColor` — and renders whatever gameplay you want. Copy an existing simple one (like `MoleRush.js`) as a starting template.
3. Register it in `games/GameComponents.js` — one line, mapping the same `component_key` to your component. This is the one step that needs a GitHub upload and redeploy.

If you'd rather not build the gameplay yourself, come back and ask — I
can keep adding games into this same structure any time; you'd only
need to do the database row yourself if you want to tweak its pricing
afterward.

## Upgrade 16 — NFSU2-style garage, audio shop, ten new cars, wet roads, landmarks

- **Ten new cars** (locked, sold in the BKC lot, ₹18,000–75,000), original names inspired by: Mercedes-AMG One (Meteor), Aston Martin Valkyrie (Vesper X), Porsche 911 GT3 RS (Rennsport), BAC Mono R (Solo R), Porsche 718 Cayman GT4 RS (Stinger), Ariel Atom 4RR (Frame 4RR), Praga Bohema (Praga), Radical RXC Turbo (Razor), Caterham Seven 620R (Sevenfold), Dallara EXP (EXP). Names/stats live in `lib/midnightBurn/physics.js`; each has an engine sound profile in `engineAudio.js`. Shapes are stylised, not exact replicas.
- **Garage/shop UI**: carousel strip, turntable with hazard ring, green spec bars, Back/Continue/Quit buttons, garage backdrop.
- **Audio shop** (Juhu Sound Garage): Layouts 1–3 (₹2,500 / 6,000 / 12,000) add +4/7/10 dB bass via a low-shelf filter in `radio.js`; speaker stacks appear in the showroom.
- **Wet reflections**: opt-in (Options → Reflections), off by default, needs a strong GPU; only active in rain.
- **Planted verges** with hedges and shrubs beside lamps (not true medians — those would block driving).
- **Fountains and casinos** on reserved plots (2046 map only).
- Limits: tested only in a software-rendered browser, never on a real GPU or played by a person.

### Upgrade 16b — extras from the second set of NFSU2 pictures
- **Convention centre** (Vashi plot, 2046 map): glass drum, stepped swept roof, lit glass front, stairs, palms, "MUMBAI CONVENTION CENTRE" sign.
- **Searchlight beams** crossing over the casinos and the convention centre.
- **"MUMBAI" hill sign** on Malabar Hill, lit, readable from both sides.
- **Highway boards** now carry a blue/red NH route shield next to the place name.
- Not done: the number plate was already on every car; the huge cantilever signs and El Norte-style icons were not copied.

### Upgrade 16c — closer to the NFSU2 night look
- Roads and pavements now keep a permanent wet-looking sheen at night (no rain needed); rain still adds more.
- Colour grade pushed toward NFSU2: deeper teal shadows, richer colour, more contrast; bloom a little stronger.
- Turn Options → Reflections ON for true mirrored buildings on a strong GPU.

### Upgrade 16d — modern Need for Speed mood (from the second reference set)
- Airborne embers: warm specks streak past the car (cyan on nitro), stronger at speed and in drifts.
- Raindrops on the camera lens in rain (bend the picture slightly).
- Wet-road micro-bumps so rain highlights glitter like the stills.
- Already present and unchanged: tyre smoke, crash sparks, motion blur, bloom, grade.
- Not done: car damage and flipping, depth-of-field, volumetric headlight fog, lit smoke.

### Upgrade 16e — crash feel, headlight beams, soft edges
- Hard hits rock the car; very hard hits at speed barrel-roll it (visual only, physics unchanged), with sparks, debris smoke and a small dent that slowly recovers. A badly hurt car smokes from the bonnet.
- Visible headlight cones in the air (stronger in rain).
- Drift smoke is now bright teal-white, as if lit by the lamps and underglow.
- Screen edges go slightly soft (lens depth-of-field feel, not true depth-based).
- Not done: real crumpled meshes, true depth-of-field, hand-modelled buildings and cars.

### Upgrade 17 — people, poles, metro, bullet train, airliners
- **Pedestrians are solid**: running one over knocks them down (they fly and lie on the road, then vanish). *(Fines were softened in Upgrade 20 — see below; the old ₹200/₹300/₹1,000 amounts no longer apply.)* Counters `career.pedHits` / `career.pedKills`.
- **Poles in roads**: ~1 in 5 metro pillars stood on a road below, and some motorway sign-gantry legs stood on other roads; both now skip/slide clear. Lamp posts that would pierce a flyover deck above are removed.
- **Metro**: 6 more lines (M6, M8 airport link, M10, M11, M12), 3–10 trains per line (was 2–5), headlamps/tail lamps with glow, visible up to 3 km.
- **Mumbai–Ahmedabad Bullet Train** (both maps): BKC – Thane – Virar – Ahmedabad terminus at the map edge; 8-car train with streamlined noses, ~300 km/h, 20 s station stops.
- **Airliners** (new `aircraft.js`): swept wings with winglets, engines, tail fin, lit windows, cheat-line, undercarriage, nav/strobe/beacon/landing lights. A landing and a take-off roughly every 70 s at each airport (narrow- and wide-body), plus 6 high-altitude flyers.
- Limits: not tested on a real GPU or by a person; aircraft are lofted primitives, not studio models.

## Upgrade 18 — double-decker buses, street furniture, full Mumbai metro list

- **Buses**: every bus is now a double-decker (two glazed decks, cream band, lit destination blind, glowing interior strips, mirrors, wheel arches). Hand-built from primitives, not studio art.
- **Street furniture**: ~85,000 items placed beside lamps along roads (benches, bus shelters, bins, kiosks, planters, bollards, hydrants, post boxes, etc.). Most have colliders, avoid buildings, water and road surfaces.
- **Metro**: Lines 1–14 (incl. 2A/2B, 4A, 7&9), Thane Ring Metro (22 stations, circular) and Navi Mumbai Multi-Line Loop (8 stations) added. Loop lines run one-directional trains on a single track.
- Honest limits: geography is approximate; the Thane ring and Navi Mumbai loop are my own layouts; tested only in a software-rendered browser, never on a real GPU or by a person.

## Upgrade 19 — roads no longer eaten by hills, piers clear of roads, all ten new cars available, rebuilt car models

- **Roads in jungles/hills**: the ground beside a road is now shaped *from* the road: each road owns a strip of land with a gentle cutting or embankment (slope limited to ~0.8 m per metre), so hillsides can't poke through the tarmac. The terrain mesh is also finer (32×32 cells per 256 m chunk, was 24×24) with a per-vertex guard, and a road that crosses another at a different height is drawn as a bridge deck instead of being buried. On the 2046 map (seed 7), the share of sampled road points where terrain rose above the road fell from about 1.4% to about 0.07% (112 of 164,182 samples, most under 0.5 m). Leftover cases: a few single-sample spikes on bank edges beside steep authored roads.
- **Piers in roads**: flyover, metro and gantry piers (and their colliders) now check for any road deck at that spot and slide clear or are skipped, so pillars no longer stand in carriageways.
- **Ten new cars are available**: every Collection car (Meteor, Vesper, Rennsport, Solo, Stinger, Frame, Praga, Razor, Seven, Exp) is owned from the start in both the city game and the Classic game, selectable in the garage. Phantom GT and Specter LMP stay story unlocks (beat Saint / Kage).
- **Rebuilt car models** (`lib/midnightBurn/carParts.js` + `cars.js`): modelled tyres with sidewall lettering; five rim styles; brake discs and calipers that follow the steering; crisp wheel arches cut from the body with dark wheel wells that follow the fender; projector/quad/round/slit headlamps and tail lamps fitted to the body surface; pillars, mirrors, a modelled cockpit (dash with lit gauges, seats, wheel, shifter, roll cage on the wide cars) seen through tinted glass; clear-coat paint with flakes (metallic, pearl, carbon weave, satin, matte) reflecting a built night-city environment; baked ambient-occlusion shading.
- **Traffic cars**: three smooth-bodied shapes (sedan, hatchback, SUV) with glass, real wheels, flush lamps, plates and door lines; Mumbai-style black-and-yellow taxis; soft contact shadows under all traffic. Rival racers use a lighter mesh (about a third of the triangles).
- Honest limits: models are built from code, not studio art; tested only in a software-rendered browser, never on a real GPU or by a person (so real frame rates are unmeasured).

### Upgrade 19b — a few more steps towards photoreal cars

- **Studio reflections**: the reflection environment now includes long softbox strips (above, at the shoulders, low beside the car, fore and aft), so glossy paint shows long clean highlights and contrast instead of a flat sheen. The garage floor reflects less so the cars stand out.
- **Body shaping**: every body gets a shoulder crease and a recessed sill line along each flank that catch the light. Wide prototype/hypercar bodies get raised, flat-topped fender humps that roof over the tyre (the dark "hole" above the front wheels is gone).
- **Panel seams**: bonnet and boot-lid outlines and bumper split lines are drawn in the paint shader as thin dark grooves that stay sharp at any zoom.
- **Tail lights** glow brighter when idle and flare when braking.
- Limits: still procedural, code-built models rather than studio art; checked only in a software-rendered browser, not on a real GPU or by a person.

## Upgrade 20 — photo-style trees, bushes, roads, bridges and ground; marginal pedestrian fines

- **Pedestrian penalty is now marginal** (`G.pedHit` in `sim.js`): about 0.1% of the driver's lifetime earnings for a slow hit, 0.25% for a fast hit and 0.4% for a kill, and the *total* of all pedestrian fines can never exceed 1.5% of lifetime earnings (`career.earned`, `career.pedFines`). A fine can never take more cash than the driver holds. The running drift bonus is trimmed by 10% (30% for a kill) instead of being wiped. Counters `career.pedHits` / `career.pedKills` are kept.
- **Trees, bushes and ground cover** (new `vegTex.js`, `vegKinds.js`, `vegetation.js`, `plants.js`): the old cube-blob trees are gone. Broadleaf trees (dome, umbrella, gulmohar, teak, banyan, peepal), pines, cypress and palms are built from tapered trunks with real branches carrying hundreds of small painted leaf/needle cards, with baked ambient occlusion and a gentle wind sway; bark is a tiling painted texture. Under them: bushes, flowering shrubs, ferns, banana clumps, long grass and boulders. Planting is deterministic per 256 m chunk (roadside verges, street trees in leafy districts, groves and dense forest in the jungle districts); trunks get colliders only when the car is near. **Plants are instanced**: each kind of plant exists as 1–3 shapes, built once at three levels of detail (near ≈ 300 triangles per tree, middle ≈ 100, far ≈ 40) and shared by the whole world; a chunk only stores where each plant stands (a matrix and a colour, with the nearest street lamp's warm glow folded into the colour). A chunk shows the near, middle or far set by its distance from the camera (230 m / 560 m, with a little hysteresis); ground cover is drawn only near, and the far set draws every second tree. A first, non-instanced version needed ~17 million triangles and 1.7 GB of memory around a forest road and crashed my test browser; the instanced version measures roughly 1–1.4 million drawn triangles and a few hundred MB of total page memory in the same views.
- **Utility poles and power lines** (`poles.js`): wooden or concrete poles with cross-arms, insulators, the odd transformer, and three sagging wires between poles along country and forest roads.
- **Roads and bridges**: concrete Jersey-barrier crash barriers on flyovers and bridges, textured concrete decks and piers, gravel shoulders beside rural roads, and a finer wet-asphalt detail texture with patches, cracks and repairs.
- **Ground colour**: outside the dense city the terrain is a patchwork of lush grass, dry grass and bare earth, with bare rock on steep slopes.
- Honest limits: everything is drawn by code (no studio models or photographs), and the game is night-only, so the plants are tuned to look right under moon and street lamps rather than the daylight shown in photo references. Tested only in a software-rendered browser, never on a real GPU or by a person, so real frame rates and the cost of dense planting on phones are unmeasured.

## Upgrade 21 — dusk and golden-hour modes, photo-style roads, buildings and metro trains, wall graffiti

- **Time of day** (new `lib/midnightBurn/open/tod.js`; wired in `render.js`, `carParts.js` `makeCityEnv`, `fx.js` grade shader, and the pause panel in `components/games/MidnightBurnCity.js`): NIGHT (exactly the old look), DUSK and GOLDEN HOUR. One table sets the sky colours, sun/moon, haze, how bright street lamps, lit windows, shop signs and headlights are, the colour grade and the glow. The sun is the same directional light the moon used, moved per time of day; the reflection environment map is rebuilt when you switch and swapped on every material. Choose it in the pause menu ("TIME" button / options) or start with `localStorage["mb-tod"]`. At golden hour the lamps are off and the windows are dim; at dusk they are on.
- **Roads** (`textures2.js` `asphaltDetail`, `roadSet`, `paveSet`; shader additions in `render.js`): the tarmac gets a world-space grit texture that stays sharp at any road width (tile 1.8 m), a bump from a mip-filtered normal map (no sparkle in the distance), patchy repairs and shine that varies across the road, and puddles in rain. The road atlas gains expansion joints on flyovers, black glossy crack-seal "tar snakes" and utility trench repairs. Pavements are now stone slabs (6 x 6 per tile) with chamfered edges, dirty grout, cracks, chipped corners, oil, gum, paan splashes and dust along the kerb, with a matching bump map.
- **Buildings** (`textures2.js` facades, `chunks3d.js` building loop, `render.js` facade materials): three residential and two office wall designs (chosen per building by a hash of its id) instead of one, each with a relief (normal) map for window recesses, sills, balcony slabs, plaster and mullions; heavier weathering (monsoon streaks, mould, peeling plaster, grime from spandrels, dusty or drawn blinds). Towers over 40 m (offices) / 55 m (flats) step back in a top tier on a ledge, and roofs get a stair bulkhead, AC condenser rows, satellite dishes and the odd phone mast with a red aviation light. The light ("lite") version keeps one design per kind and skips the relief maps. Fixed: painted adverts on building walls (PAINT, CEMENT...) were mirrored.
- **Metro and bullet trains** (new `trains.js`; trains block in `render.js`): cars are shaped tubes (skirt, straight walls, rounded shoulders, crowned roof) with a painted side map (door leaves with their own windows, tinted glass with a sky reflection, destination boards, grime), cabins lit from inside with passenger silhouettes and straps (an emissive map, dimmed in daylight), the line colour as a stripe, a raked cab front on metros and a long beak with a dark visor on the bullet train, bogies with wheels, underfloor boxes, roof AC units, and a pantograph on motor cars. The line colour is a vertex colour, so one texture serves every line (two side maps in all).
- **Graffiti** (new `graffiti.js`; placement in `chunks3d.js`, `GRAF` rates): a painted spray-paint atlas of 11 street pieces (bubble tags, a stencil crown, a marker tag, a heart, rainbow lettering...) and three "MIDNIGHT BURN MUMBAI" crew pieces. About 1 in 75 quiet low walls (no shopfront band) and 3% of tall bridge piers get a street piece; roughly 30 walls map-wide get a crew piece (`GRAF.mb`). Nothing is placed on shopfronts or glass towers.
- Honest limits: everything is drawn by code, not photographs or studio models; checked only in a software-rendered browser, never on a real GPU or by a person, so real frame rates and the exact look on a real monitor are unmeasured. Extra GPU memory from the new textures is roughly 100-150 MB on the full version.


## Upgrade 22 — smooth flyover ramps, no obstacles on roads, "Mumbai Drift", cleaner city, new happy music and intro, light admin, Legend Pass locked in

- **Heights, ramps and piers** (`lib/midnightBurn/open/roads.js`): a new solver (`levelCrossings`: pin, lift, relax) gives every place where two roads cross at least 7.4 m of clear headroom or makes them merge into one smooth level (never a step); the metro is built last so it always clears the roads (analysis: 0 metro points under 7.9 m over a road, 0 at the same level as a road). Flyover ramps are rebuilt (18 ramps, steepest 12.8 %), link roads that came out steeper than 16 % are dropped and re-fitted, and piers slide clear of any road. Measured with the node analysis scripts only; never driven by a person.
- **Dead ends**: every road end is now either linked to a nearby road (candidates tried in order, rejected if they run along another road or into an airport) or closed with a red-and-white barricade with amber flashers and a "no entry" sign (`world.js` closure walls, drawn in `chunks3d.js`). 25 interior ends and about 350 map-edge stubs get barricades.
- **Nothing standing in the road**: buildings, lamps, furniture, festival sites and landmarks are all tested against the exact road footprint (`rectClear`, `planSites`, `landmarks3d.js` `roadHit`/`spot`/`fit`). Gateway, Taj, CST (shrinks or moves until clear), the Vashi arch (legs beside the widest nearby road, at deck height) and the Sea Link / Atal Setu towers (legs now beside the deck, not on it) were corrected. Last analysis: 0 of 121 767 buildings, 0 lamps and 0 furniture pieces on a road.
- **Cleaner Mumbai**: all paan stains removed (`textures2.js`); in the 2046 map the walls, roads and pavements are scrubbed (`setTextureClean`, called from `render.js`).
- **Name**: the map is "Mumbai Drift" everywhere (`MidnightBurnCity.js`, `MumbaiStreetRacer.js`, `MidnightBurnIntro.js`) and the card text in the database (`supabase/migration_077_mumbai_drift.sql`).
- **Music** (`stormTracks.js`, `music.js`, `radio.js`, `introScore.js`): everything is original and synthesised live (no recordings). All 60 generated tracks and the named stations moved to bright major keys with bouncy bass, syncopated drums, marimba/pluck hooks and open, driving chord loops, aiming at the cheerful drift-movie mood; the intro score is in C major (checked by an offline key analysis: build and drop both detect as C major). No melody or lyric from any existing song is used.
- **Intro film** (`MidnightBurnCity.js` intro director, `MidnightBurnIntro.js` captions, `introScore.js`): golden-hour drone sweep round the car crossing the Sea Link, a cruise under the metro viaduct on the elevated highway, dusk at HQ, then the night drop with the "MIDNIGHT BURN / MUMBAI DRIFT" title. Shot spots are found from the map at run time (`introSpots`). New helpers: `G.placeOnRoad(roadId, pointIndex, speed)` in `sim.js` and `view.setTimeOfDay(name, temporary)` in `render.js` (the intro's time-of-day changes are not saved to the player's setting and are restored afterwards).
- **Street furniture and cars** (`chunks3d.js` furniture switch; `render.js` `makeHero`): benches now have slats and cast-iron frames, bins have rims, lids and labels, hydrants have nozzles and caps, phone boxes have glazing bars, kiosks have stocked shelves and scalloped awnings, transformer cabinets have doors and louvres, and each piece gets a slight weathered colour variation. Hero cars get a second, tighter contact shadow so they sit on the road, and traffic cars now use a lacquered clear-coat paint (glossy reflections along the body and roof) plus a tighter contact shadow; the traffic cars' wheels now have five alloy spokes, and the auto-rickshaw is rebuilt (open sides with a seat visible, black canvas roof on slim pillars, slanted windscreen, handlebars, mudguards, plate).
- **Admin panel** (`app/admin/*`, `components/admin/*`, `app/globals.css` `.admin-light`): a light theme (white cards, dark text, larger type, clear focus rings) and a friendlier menu where every link says in plain words what it is for.
- **Contrast**: the shared "dim" text colour is lighter (`tailwind.config.js` `textDim`), faint `text-textDim/NN` variants are gone, and the pricing page uses larger, brighter text.
- **Legend Pass**: Midnight Burn, Operation Blacksite, Ashtide (Kingdoms of Ash) and Wrath of Olympus are locked to Legend Pass in code (`lib/flagshipSlugs.js` `LEGEND_FLAGSHIP_SLUGS`, enforced in `lib/games.js` `shapeGame`, so a wrong database edit can never make them free) and in the database (`supabase/migration_078_legend_flagships.sql`). The pricing page lists them first with a "FLAGSHIP" tag.
- **To apply**: run `migration_077_mumbai_drift.sql` and `migration_078_legend_flagships.sql` in the Supabase SQL editor.
- Honest limits: all art and music is made by code; everything was checked in a software-rendered test browser, by number-crunching scripts and by an offline audio analysis, never on a real GPU, by a person playing, or by ear. Real frame rates, how the music sounds, and the exact look on a real monitor are unmeasured.

## Upgrade 23 — fewer files per folder, hero-car ladder, automatic day clock, skywalks and subways, realistic people, live "Most Played", Palm Beach Road drag start

**1. New folder layout (so GitHub's 100-files-per-upload limit is never a problem).** Nothing about the website or the games changed; files were only moved and their `import` lines re-pointed (checked: all 717 local imports resolve, and every page and game screen compiles to the same code as before the move). Old → new:

| Old place | New place |
| --- | --- |
| `components/games/*` (casual games, `GameComponents.js`, `GameRunner.js`, `FullscreenStage.js`, `PremiumShell.js` …) | `games/` |
| `components/games/MidnightBurn*.js`, `MumbaiStreetRacer.js` | `midnightburn/` |
| `lib/midnightBurn/*` (physics, cars, music, the open-world engine in `open/`) | `midnightburn/engine/` |
| `components/games/OperationBlacksite.js` + `lib/blacksite/*` | `blacksite/` (screens) + `blacksite/engine/` |
| `components/games/WrathOfOlympus.js` + `lib/olympus/*` | `olympus/` + `olympus/engine/` |
| `components/games/KingdomsOfAsh.js` + `lib/townscape/*` | `kingdoms/` + `kingdoms/engine/` |
| `lib/cinematic/*` | unchanged (shared by several games) |

File counts per folder now (everything inside, including sub-folders): app 88, components 61, lib 46, games 61, midnightburn 47, blacksite 20, olympus 13, kingdoms 7: every one is under 100, so each can be uploaded in one go.
**When you upload this version to GitHub, delete the old folders `components/games`, `lib/midnightBurn`, `lib/blacksite`, `lib/olympus` and `lib/townscape` first** (otherwise the website still builds, but the old copies sit there unused). The site stays modular: to update one casual game upload just `games/ThatGame.js`; to update a flagship upload only its own folder (`midnightburn/`, `blacksite/`, `olympus/` or `kingdoms/`). `tailwind.config.js` was told to look in the new folders (otherwise styles would vanish). The `supabase/` folder holds 82 files now; the SQL files there are only ever pasted into the Supabase SQL editor, so after 100 you never need to upload the old ones again (only new ones).

**2. "Most Played Games" on the leaderboard now updates by itself** (`lib/games.js` `getPopularGames`, `app/api/popular-games/route.js`, `components/MostPlayedLive.js`, `app/leaderboard/page.js`). The old list counted from a capped query (so it froze once a game had many scores). Plays are now counted in the database itself, the page is never cached, and an open leaderboard page refreshes the list every 30 seconds and when you come back to the tab. **To apply: run `supabase/migration_079_game_play_totals.sql` once in the Supabase SQL editor** (it also back-fills the counts from existing data). Until it is run, the site falls back to the old way, so nothing breaks.

**3. Hero cars for every career (new and old).** A career starts with the three normal cars plus two hero cars (Rennsport GT and Stinger GT4). The other eight unlock as you level up: level 2 Sevenfold 620, 3 Frame 4RR, 4 Prodigy EXP, 5 Praha Bolt, 6 Solo R, 7 Razor RXC, 8 Meteor One, 10 Vesper X. A toast announces each gift. Careers saved before this upgrade are topped up automatically on load (they receive every hero car their level has earned), and a car, once owned, is never taken away. The career tab has a "YOUR CARS" switcher (`midnightburn/MidnightBurnCity.js`, `midnightburn/engine/physics.js`, `open/sim.js`).

**4. The hour of the day is automatic.** Sunset → dusk → a long night → pre-dawn → dawn glow → sunset again, in a smooth loop of about 15 minutes of play (timetable: `TOD_CYCLE`; every game session opens at sunset) (`midnightburn/engine/open/tod.js` timetable, `render.js` clock). Players can no longer choose it (the pause screen only shows what time it currently is, e.g. "🌅 SUNSET", "DUSK", "NIGHT"). The reflections of the sky are rebuilt once per change, at the point where the old look has faded out.

**5. Palm Beach Road: the iconic midnight drag** is a level-1 event (`open/content.js` `drag-palm`), and a new career can begin there instead of at the home garage (a choice on the start screen: "THE HOME GARAGE" or "PALM BEACH ROAD: THE MIDNIGHT DRAG"). The old "Vashi Bridge" drag keeps its place under that name.

**6. People no longer walk across roads at street level.** `open/walkways.js` finds every place a pavement meets a road. Where the road is wide enough and the approaches are clear, the city builds a **skywalk** (a covered footbridge with a long stair on each side, lights and railings) or a **subway** (a lit entrance building on each side; walkers go in and come out on the far side). At every other crossing the pavement simply stops for pedestrians, who turn back. In the 2046 map this is about 490 crossings with a structure (roughly 320 skywalks and 160 subways) and about 4,100 closed crossings. Trees, lamps and street furniture keep clear of them, the stairs are solid, and the crowd (`open/crowd.js`) climbs the stairs, crosses at 5.2 m height and vanishes into subway doors.

**7. More realistic people** (`open/people.js`, used by `open/crowd.js`). Eight kinds of pedestrian with proper proportions and round limbs: man in jeans and shirt, office man, man in T-shirt and shorts, woman in kurta with dupatta, woman in a saree, woman in jeans with a ponytail and sling bag, older man in kurta-pyjama with a white topi, and a schoolchild with a backpack. Faces have eyes, nose, lips and ears; hair is styled; shoes and sandals are separate. The walk bends the knees and elbows, swings the arms against the legs, turns the shoulders and bobs; women in long clothes take shorter steps. A soft shadow patch sits under every person. Each person is about 1,500 triangles and everybody shares a handful of draw calls.

**8. Photo-realism pass on Midnight Burn.** Towers and glass use natural steel, silver and grey-blue instead of neon cyan/violet/magenta (`open/world.js` palettes); the colour grade, contrast and glow are calmer (`open/tod.js`); dusk and golden hour are a little brighter; every building gets a soft contact shadow at its foot (`open/chunks3d.js`, `render.js` `mats.ao`).

**Honest limits.** All art and music is made by code (no photographs, no recordings), so "photo-realistic" here means "much more natural", not a photograph. Everything was checked in a software-rendered test browser, by number-crunching scripts and in a compile check of every page, never on a real graphics card and never by a person playing. In particular unmeasured: real frame rates (people are heavier than before: about 1,500 triangles each; if a low-end laptop struggles, lower the crowd size in `render.js` `createCrowd(... max: 760)`), how the day fades feel in real time, how the new colours look on your monitor, and the Supabase side of migration 079 (written carefully, not run against a real database). The subway is an illusion (walkers walk into one entrance and appear at the other); a subway or skywalk is built only at crossings that have clear pavement on both approaches, so the remaining crossings are closed to pedestrians instead.


## Upgrade 24 — Operation Blacksite looks much closer to Counter-Strike

Everything below is inside `blacksite/engine/` (plus nothing else): the rules, bots, buy menu, scores and the other games were not touched. Blacksite now has 23 files in total, still well under 100.

**1. Natural, gritty ground and walls (new file `surfaces.js`).** Paving, dirt, concrete, wood and steel are now drawn by code with real-world detail: stones with uneven edges and chipped corners, tiny gravel, tyre-and-boot scuffs, hairline cracks, rust and oil stains, plus a "bumpiness" map and a "shininess" map so light slides over each surface the way it does in real life (wet asphalt really shines at night, dry sandstone stays dull). Every floor tile also gets a slow, gentle colour drift so the repeating pattern is never obvious.

**2. Weathered building fronts (new file `skins.js`).** Each wall now has sun-bleached patches, rain streaks, soot under windows, damp climbing from the ground, a stone plinth, chips where the plaster fell off to show brick, repaint patches, hairline cracks and old bullet pocks. The three daytime maps (Bazaar, Harbor, Quarter) got calmer, dustier colours and a hazy sky instead of the old bright cartoon palette. The night map (Foundry) uses real-size bricks (about 25 x 8 cm; before they were twice that size) and a less cracked asphalt.

**3. A calmer "Counter-Strike" look.** One shared colour grade (slightly desaturated, firm contrast, warm highlights and cool shadows, soft vignette, fine film grain), gentler glow, softer sun and sky, and little blob shadows under crates and barrels so nothing floats. The three daytime maps each got their own mood (`maps/bazaar.js`, `harbor.js`, `quarter.js`).

**4. Realistic weapons (new file `guns.js`).** Every gun is now a shaped model rather than a pile of boxes: pistol, knife, an AK-style rifle (wood furniture, curved magazine), an M4-style rifle, an MP5-style submachine gun and a thumbhole sniper rifle, with stamped-steel details, rails, sights and rubber. In first person (`viewmodel.js`) the gun now sits low on the right, tilted like in Counter-Strike, with gloved hands that actually hold the grip and handguard and sleeves in the team colour. The old glowing strip on top of each gun is gone.

**5. Soldiers (`soldier.js`).** Muted, real-looking uniforms (olive and brown for the attackers, navy for the defenders), a finer camouflage pattern, tamer team-colour trim, and the same new guns in their hands. Name tags above teammates are smaller and quieter.

**6. Hits and shots feel physical (`fx.js`).** Bullet holes are now drawn ones (dark pit, chipped rim, hairline cracks, random rotation) instead of black discs; hits throw a puff of dust in the colour of the wall and a few flying chips, and hot sparks only appear on metal. Muzzle flashes are a jagged star with a white-hot centre instead of a round blob.

**Quality settings.** Nothing changed in the settings screen. Low/Medium/High/Ultra still decide shadow sharpness and edge smoothing. The new ground and wall art is made once when a map loads (about a second in total on the test machine) and costs nothing extra while playing, except the new post-processing grade, which is the same single pass as before.

**Honest limits.** All art is made by code (no photographs, no image files), so "photo-realistic" here means "much more natural and Counter-Strike-like", not a photograph. It was checked only in a software-rendered browser on a test machine (never on a real graphics card) with a full match started and bots playing, and no page errors; nobody has played it with a mouse yet, so colours, brightness and speed on your laptop may still need a small tweak. The night map has strong red light near the attackers' start (from the "T SPAWN" lamp); that was already the design.

## Upgrade 25 — Midnight Burn: much smoother on everyday laptops, a bigger and clearer GPS, cars and upgrades that are worth buying, no more trees on roads

Everything below is inside `midnightburn/` (the open-world game "Midnight Burn", also used by the free Mumbai Street mode). The older circuit game, Operation Blacksite and the other games were not touched. File counts stay well under 100 per folder (one new file, `engine/open/slice.js`).

**1. Smooth on an average laptop (the main fix).**
- *Why it stuttered.* Two things caused the lag. First, the picture was drawn at a fixed, rich quality (glow, soft shadows, sharp edges) that only a strong graphics card can keep up with; even "Auto" only started from your screen size and then lowered sharpness. Second, every time you drove into new streets the game built the whole new city block in one go: on the test machine that froze single frames for 70 to 170 milliseconds (a visible "hitch"), and the browser's own tidy-up of the leftovers added more pauses.
- *Picture quality now has 8 automatic steps* (FEATHER, LIGHT, EASY, BALANCED, GOOD, HIGH, VERY HIGH, ULTRA). Each step changes how sharp the picture is, how often and how finely shadows are redrawn, the glow, the lens effects, how far the city is built around you, and how many plants and people are drawn. The game first guesses a step from your graphics card and processor (an ordinary laptop chip starts low), then checks your frame rate every half second and moves down at once if it is slow, or tries one step up after a calm stretch (and goes back if it does not hold). Your best step is remembered for next time. Pause ▸ Options now explains this and shows the current step; "SAVER" is the lightest fixed setting, HD / QHD / 4K still lock the sharpness for strong cards.
- *New city blocks are built a few milliseconds at a time.* The nearer the missing block, the more time it gets, so fast driving still keeps ahead of you. Measured on the test machine, the longest single stall while entering new streets fell from 70–170 ms to about 5–35 ms, and driving flat out for 20 seconds produced no frame above about 28 ms of game-code time.
- *Less leftover for the browser to tidy.* Building a block no longer creates tens of megabytes of throw-away lists (they are borrowed from a small pool and given back, giving exactly the same shapes: checked vertex by vertex), and a block's own copy of its shape numbers is dropped once the graphics card has it, which also lowers memory use. If the system ever resets the graphics card, the city blocks are simply rebuilt.
- The auto-quality ignores the time spent building blocks, so it does not lower your picture quality just because you drove into new streets.

**2. A bigger, clearer GPS (`maps.js`, HUD in `MidnightBurnCity.js`).** The round map grew from a fixed 168 pixels to about 30% of the screen height (216 pixels in a 720-pixel-high window, 324 on a full-HD screen, with sensible limits), a fifth bigger again during a race, and it is drawn sharply on high-resolution screens. Roads are drawn as crisp lines with outlines (side streets, main roads, motorways, flyovers and tunnels each look different), the route is a bright line with a dark edge and little white pulses that run in the direction you must drive, and the map turns with your car. A banner above it shows an arrow, the distance and the next move ("TURN LEFT", "BEAR RIGHT", "U-TURN", "DESTINATION" or "FINISH"); it shows "WRONG WAY — TURN AROUND" if you drive against the route; in a race the footer shows the distance to the next checkpoint. The zoom follows your speed (close when slow, wide when fast); press **Z** to pin it close / medium / wide / auto (remembered). Things that are off the edge are shown by little arrows on the rim.

**3. Cars and upgrades are now worth it (`physics.js`, `sim.js`, shop screens).**
- *Every car keeps its own upgrades.* Engine, tyres and nitrous are bought for the car you are driving and stay with it. A career saved before this keeps what it paid for: its upgrades are copied onto every car it already owns, once.
- *Each upgrade level is a real step* (engine: +9% launch power and +2.4% top speed per level; tyres: +6% grip and +3.5% steering; nitrous: +9% kick and 15% longer). Before, a level gave only 3.5–7%. The shop now shows exactly what the next level will give ("Top speed 251 → 257 km/h · launch +8%") and a rating (a stock Kestrel is 100) with a class letter D to S.
- *Faster cars cost more to tune* (the same percentage is worth more on a quicker car), but they also climb higher.
- *Hero cars are bought with cash.* Before, they were simply handed over at each level (their prices were never charged). Now the level unlocks the car in the Car Lot and cash buys it: Sevenfold ₹3,000 (level 2), Frame ₹5,000 (3), Prodigy ₹7,500 (4), Praha Bolt ₹10,500 (5), Solo R ₹14,000 (6), Razor ₹18,500 (7), Meteor One ₹25,000 (8), Vesper X ₹35,000 (10). The two start cars (Rennsport, Stinger) and the two ladder rewards (Phantom, Specter) stay free. Cars you already own are never taken away.
- *A clear ladder.* Stock ratings now run Kestrel/Brute/Gecko about 100, Stinger 110, Rennsport 113, Sevenfold 115, Frame 122, Phantom 124, Prodigy 126, Praha 129, Solo 136, Razor 139, Meteor 143, Specter 141 and Vesper 153; full upgrades add about 35 more to a starter car and about 50 to a top car (a stock Kestrel tops out near 198 km/h, a fully upgraded one near 228 km/h). A fully upgraded starter car roughly equals a mid-ladder car, so upgrading and buying are both good choices.
- Rival drivers, boss duels and the older circuit game keep their old numbers, so their difficulty did not move. The car lot is now sorted from slowest to fastest and shows prices before a car unlocks.

**4. Trees on the road (`plants.js`).** Trees were kept off the tarmac, but big crowns could still hang over it. A tree now stands back from the road edge by its full crown size plus a small margin (and from flyover decks), and big-crowned kinds beside streets are swapped for slim ones. Checked over a quarter of the whole city (about 42,000 trees) against the road surface the car actually drives on: 0 trunks on a road and 0 crowns touching a road (the count of trees dropped by only about 1%).

**Honest limits.** All checks ran in a software-rendered browser on a test machine, never on a real graphics card, and nobody has played this version, so the exact frame rate on your laptop is unknown; the automatic steps are there to find it for you within seconds (the lowest step draws roughly half as many objects as the highest: about 870 draw calls and 0.65 million triangles against about 1,620 and 1.75 million in the test scene). If it ever still feels choppy, pick SAVER in Pause ▸ Options. Loading the city at the very start still takes a while (it builds the whole map once). Hero-car purchases and per-car upgrades change the in-game economy, so an old career may feel richer in cars than before and a little poorer in cash.

## Upgrade 26 — Midnight Burn: smooth flyover ramps, solid trees, turning searchlights · Wrath of Olympus: champions, gods, secrets in the fog, Wonder victory

### Midnight Burn
- **Ramps up to flyovers and elevated roads** — the ramp now rises in one smooth curve and the car is always placed on the road surface it is actually driving on, so it no longer drops to the street below. Checked by driving every ramp in the city in a test (the only leftover case is a test-script artefact, not something a player can hit).
- **Trees are solid** — every tree now has a solid trunk at every graphics quality, and when the game lowers or raises quality the solid trunks follow the trees (no invisible trunks left behind, no trees you can drive through).
- **Searchlights now move** — the sky beams over the casinos slowly sweep round the sky, cross and part, have soft edges, fade as they climb and only burn at night.

### Wrath of Olympus (a big change, in the spirit of Age of Mythology)
- **Pick a champion before the war** — five main characters, each with a different style: **Achilles** (front-line bruiser: Rage, Call the Myrmidons, boosts nearby soldiers), **Odysseus** (strategist: richer start, faster gathering, sees every secret on the minimap, Trojan Ruse, Eyes of the Fox), **Atalanta** (archer: stronger archers, Volley of Arrows, Swift Foot), **Hippolyta** (cavalry: cheaper, tougher horsemen, Amazon Charge, War Cry), **Perseus** (controller and monster hunter: Gorgon's Gaze turns enemies to stone, Winged Sandals leap). Each has two abilities (the second unlocks at level 2) and decides which three divine powers you may call from a Temple.
- **Your champion grows** — levels 1–5 from fights, shrines, dens and relics. If they fall you can bring them back from a Temple or Town Center for gold. The enemy has a champion too and the computer uses it.
- **Choose a god each age** — entering the Classical and Heroic ages you pick one of two minor gods (Athena or Hermes; Apollo or Hephaestus). Each changes your army and unlocks a myth creature (Minotaur, Centaur, Bronze Giant) at the Temple.
- **The fog now has a purpose** — it hides Oracle shrines (free favor, experience and a big lit-up area), monster dens (wolves, satyrs, cyclops) that guard gold and experience, and four sacred relics (Spear, Aegis fragment, Golden Fleece, Cup of Nectar) that give your whole army a permanent bonus when your champion carries them to your Temple. Faint "?" circles on the minimap are rumours. The fog itself looks different: drifting dark mist for unknown land, calm dimming for land you have seen, a faint golden edge at the border.
- **A second way to win** — build a Wonder (Classical Age) and keep it standing for four minutes. The enemy can try the same, and the computer will attack your Wonder if you build one. War horns and warnings tell you when an attack is coming.
- Computer opponent: has a champion, picks gods, builds a Temple and myth units, builds a Wonder when rich, and no longer walls itself in (a bug found while testing).
- Code is in `olympus/engine/`: `heroes.js` (champion rules), `neutrals.js` (shrines, dens, relics), `models2.js` (new 3D characters and map secrets), plus changes in `defs.js`, `sim.js`, `ai.js`, `mapgen.js`, `engine.js`, `render.js` and `olympus/WrathOfOlympus.js`.

### Honest limits
Everything was tested only with scripts and a software-rendered browser, never on a real graphics card, and nobody has played it yet. Balance (how strong each champion, god and relic is) is a first guess and will need tuning once real players try it.

## Upgrade 27 — Wrath of Olympus: a much more realistic look
- **Textured buildings:** clay roof tiles, weathered plaster with timber beams, cut stone, veined marble and wood planks, all painted by the game itself (no image files). Houses and Town Centers now look like timber-framed Greek homes.
- **Real-looking people:** every soldier, archer, horseman, citizen and champion is rebuilt with rounded limbs, a face, bronze chest armour and greaves, Corinthian helmets with crests, leather-strip skirts, cloaks and painted round shields. Horses and wolves are rounded too. Achilles is gold with a red crest and cloak, Odysseus has a pilos cap and beard, Atalanta a ponytail and quiver, Hippolyta rides a white horse, Perseus has a winged helmet and mirror shield. The Minotaur, Bronze Giant, Cyclops, satyrs and centaurs are rebuilt the same way.
- **Better world:** the ground has grass and dirt detail with smooth hills, trees have lumpy leafy crowns, bronze and gold now shine, and the camera sits a little lower so you see figures from the side as well as the top. You can zoom in closer, and units are drawn about 25% larger so they are readable.
- **Speed:** each detailed figure is welded into a few parts so large armies still run smoothly.
- Honest limit: this is still a 3D game drawn live in the browser, so it looks richer and more solid but not like a pre-drawn picture from a 2000s strategy game. Only tested in a software-rendered browser, never on a real graphics card; I could not measure real-laptop speed. If it feels slow, tell me and I will add a "simple graphics" switch.

## Upgrade 28 — Midnight Burn: ramps, flyovers and invisible walls
You sent a video where the car, doing 70+ km/h on a wide street under a flyover, suddenly stopped dead with sparks and nothing visible in the way. Driving test cars through the whole city found two real causes, both now fixed:
- **Invisible things hanging over the street (the main one).** Lamp posts and pillars that stand on a flyover were solid all the way down, so a car driving *underneath* a flyover could hit a post that was several metres above its head. It also happened with pillars whose foot sits on a hill above a sunken road. Cars now only touch a solid object if it is really at the car's height. Over 500 such spots across the city (about 200 sitting right in a lane) are gone; the test now finds 6 left, all roadside bits at the very edge of a road.
- **Clipping the top of a pillar on a ramp.** A flyover's pillar stops 1.3 m under the deck. A car climbing a ramp rides a little lower than the deck 3 m ahead of it, so it clipped the top of the pillar under its own lane and was thrown to a halt. The margin is fixed.
- **Snapped down off the flyover.** On a wide flyover, a car in an outer lane was marked down by the game's "which road am I on?" check, so any smaller road passing a metre or two underneath (a link road, a street) won, and the car was dropped onto it, under the deck, in front of a wall. The check now looks mainly at which surface is at the car's own height.
- Result of the test drives (about 2,300 runs along every raised stretch of road, in three lanes): trouble spots fell by about two thirds, wall hits on ramps fell by more than half, and a car driven up the Western Express ramps in the real game build reached the flyover in about 8 seconds at 90 km/h.
- Honest limits: all of this was tested with computer-driven cars and a software-drawn browser, never a real graphics card and never by a person playing. The ramps that join a flyover from the side still pass partly under its edge for the last ~50 m before they rise level with it (you may see the car go "into" the underside there); I have not redrawn those joins.


## Upgrade 29 — Midnight Burn: scraping a barrier no longer kills your speed

**What you reported:** a second video where the car suddenly lost nearly all its speed (129 km/h down to 6 km/h, later 119 down to 13) beside a flyover, with nothing visible in the way.

**What was wrong (found by re-creating it):**
- Whenever the car touched a wall or barrier, even just brushing along it, the game removed a fixed slice of the car's sideways speed on every single frame. A light graze at 138 km/h left the car at about 8 km/h within two seconds, and the bump also turned the nose, so the car ended up sideways. It felt exactly like hitting an invisible wall.
