- Separately, the flyover's edge barrier could still stand across the lanes of a ramp that joins it (and the other way round), which is a second possible source of an invisible wall at the mouth of a ramp.

**What changed:**
- The loss from rubbing along a wall now depends on how hard the car actually pushes into it, plus a light drag. Test: a 10° graze at 138 km/h now leaves about 100 km/h after 2 s (it was 8 km/h); a head-on hit still stops the car.
- A ramp or link road and the road it joins no longer treat each other's edge barrier as solid. Dead-end barricades are untouched.

**Honest limits:** checked with computer-driven test cars (a wall-scrape experiment and every ramp driven at several lane positions and speeds) and not by a person playing on a real graphics card. I could not find the exact spot in the city from your video, so I cannot promise the very same stretch is now smooth. Ramps that join a flyover from the side still pass partly under the flyover's edge for their last stretch; that is unchanged.

### Upgrade 29 (part 2) — Midnight Burn: ramps that join a flyover from the side

**What was wrong:** the ramps that peel off a flyover started in the middle of its outer lane and only slowly moved sideways while sinking, so for their first 40–70 m the driving surface of the ramp lay up to about 4 m *below* the flyover's own slab directly above it. The car was driving "inside" the flyover's edge.

**What changed:**
- A ramp now starts level with the flyover, partly on its outer lane and partly beside it, and moves out to the side straight away, so the slab above never overlaps a lower ramp surface by more than about half a metre.
- The ramp's top end has a short barrier across the part that sticks out past the flyover's edge, so a very fast car on the outer edge of the ramp can no longer slip past the end and drop off the side.
- When a flyover had been raised after its ramp was planned, the ramp top and the link street it connects to are now raised with it (the ramp may climb up to about 12 % in that case).
- Test: the measured stretch where a ramp runs under its flyover fell from 77 spots to 18 minor ones (mostly half a metre), every ramp top meets the road it joins at the same height, and every ramp driven up and down at 58, 108 and 144 km/h at three lane positions had no falls and no hops.

**Honest limits:** 13 ramps now exist where there were 16 (three ramps whose flyover had been raised by several metres could not be fitted without being too steep, so they were left out). Two long ramps near the east of the map (ramp_atal_446 and ramp_atal_582) still run about 1 m under their flyover's edge for a stretch. All of this was checked with computer-driven test cars, not by a person on a real graphics card.

## Upgrade 30 — east ramps, faster loading, Blacksite auto quality, new Featured section

- **Ramps (Midnight Burn):** the two long east-side ramps (`ramp_atal_446`, `ramp_atal_582`) now stay level with their flyover's edge. `atal_446` is within about half a metre at its very first point; `atal_582` is within about 1 m at its first point only (it was about 1 m for the first ~40 m). Both ramps' first metres are fenced by the end-cap barrier.
- **Midnight Burn loading:** road look-ups and the building of walls/obstacles were made cheaper (no new objects per lookup, early rejects). The city is built about 25% faster in our slow test setup and is exactly the same city (checked with a fingerprint of the whole world). Texture noise was made cheaper with identical output. Nothing visual was reduced.
- **Operation Blacksite:** new default quality setting **Auto**. It picks a starting level from the graphics chip/memory/cores, then lowers or raises the picture sharpness on the fly to hold smooth play (never below a sensible floor), and caps the frame rate at 75 so fast screens don't waste power. Manual levels are still there. The saved choice key changed to `blacksite-quality-v2`, so everyone starts on Auto. New file: `lib/gpuTier.js`.
- **Homepage Featured section:** `components/FlagshipBanner.js` + new `components/FlagshipArt.js` — a wide hero card for Midnight Burn and three cards below, each with a drawn night scene (pure SVG, no image downloads) in the game's own colour, short feature chips, and a hover lift.
- Test limits: only software-rendered headless browsers were available — no real graphics card and no person playing. Auto quality's live adjusting could not be exercised there.

## Upgrade 31 — Midnight Burn, further speed-up

- Every place in the road code that asks "which roads are near this spot?" (ground height, "is something standing under/over this spot", ramp laying, nearest road) now throws away far-off road pieces with a cheap distance test before doing the exact maths. The exact maths is unchanged for everything that was kept, so the city is identical (same whole-world fingerprint before and after).
- City build time in our slow test machine: about 57 s → 50 s (a further ~12%, ~30% less than before Upgrade 30). Real computers with a graphics card build it far faster. In a software-only browser the picture-texture drawing dominates, so the total there barely moves.
- Not changed: how the game looks. Nothing was reduced.

## Upgrade 32 — Midnight Burn: TOKYO DRIFT (second map)

- **New map "Tokyo Drift"**, chosen from the Midnight Burn menu (Mumbai Drift / Tokyo Drift). Each map keeps its own save slot, cash (¥ in Tokyo), and best times. Mumbai is unchanged (same city fingerprint as before).
- **Geography:** Tokyo plus Yokohama, Kawasaki, Chiba, Saitama, Tama, Shonan and the Boso coast — 68 districts, Tokyo Bay with reclaimed land (Haneda, Odaiba, Daikoku, Umihotaru), the Sumida / Arakawa / Edo / Tama rivers with bridges, hills, and Haneda Airport. Distances are compressed the same way Mumbai's were so it plays like a big city, not a map of Japan at scale.
- **Roads:** the Shuto Expressway (C1 loop, routes 1–7), Kanjo, Wangan, Aqua-Line, Rainbow / Bay / Gate / Tsubasa bridges, Keihin roads, Yokosuka, Shonan, Boso and the touge (Yabitsu, Takao, Okutama, Omekaido, Miura, Daikoku…). Streets fill the rest.
- **Metro:** Yamanote loop, Tokyo Metro lines, Keikyu airport line, Yurikamome, Tokaido / Tohoku Shinkansen and more private lines.
- **Landmarks (42):** Tokyo Tower, Skytree, Rainbow Bridge, Shibuya Crossing, Senso-ji, Shinjuku towers, Tokyo Station, Daikoku PA, Odaiba Ferris wheel, Big Sight, Landmark Tower, refineries, Great Buddha…
- **Events (~70):** circuits, sprints, knock-out, drags (iconic: Wangan Midnight Drag), duels, drift zones, speed traps, jobs, shops.
- **Look:** Japanese shop signs, billboards, neon blade signs, Tokyo gantry boards, Tokyo graffiti, no auto-rickshaws (kei-style cars instead).
- **Music:** a separate 36-track radio — Lo-fi, Synthwave, Chill electronic — selectable with its own saved station.
- **Test limits:** headless software rendering only (no real graphics card, nobody playing, no real ears on the music — checked by offline render for level and clipping). Tokyo builds in ~65 s in the slow test setup (Mumbai ~50 s).

### Upgrade 32 (part 2) — Tokyo fixes after testing
- **Roads off the map:** seven Tokyo routes (Takao, Okutama, Kanetsu, r4–r6, kk4) ran past the map edge; they are now cut cleanly at the edge, and the far west (Takao, Okutama, Sagamiko) was nudged in so those places and their sprint events are on the map.
- **Ramps:** every ramp top that was wrongly closed by a "road closed" barricade is opened (also fixes 1 Mumbai ramp); a ramp buried in a hill is removed. All 100 Tokyo ramps (and 13 Mumbai) now drive cleanly up and down in the test.
- **C1 loop:** the closed Inner Circular loop no longer sinks to ground level at its seam.
- **Haneda:** smaller, squarer island; the Tama River now reaches the sea; airport and runways resized to fit.
- **Tokyo Tower** made taller and wider so it rises above the nearby skyscrapers. **Skytree** checked from a distance: shape looks right.
- Test limits: Tokyo Tower could not be shown whole in a screenshot because dense towers hide it from every test camera spot; full-road driving test shows small hops on some link streets, same kind and rate as Mumbai.

## Upgrade 33 (part 1) — Midnight Burn: the Blacklist (Most Wanted-style career)

- **Fifteen rivals per city, #15 up to the boss at #1.** Mumbai has a rough street-racing crew (dock gangs, bridge kings, the Monsoon Phantom, the Midnight Sultan); Tokyo has a clean drift-racing crew (touge masters, Wangan and Daikoku legends, the Emperor of the Wangan). No police yet.
- **Milestones:** to challenge a rival you must (1) finish two races on their turf, (2) reach one skill goal (wins, gold medals, landmarks, top speed, drift score or near misses), and (3) have beaten the rival below. Each milestone pops up as it is reached and a "CHALLENGE OPEN" message appears when the rival is ready.
- **Rewards:** beat a rival to take their place on the list, a cash and XP bounty, and (for nine of them) their car. The old rule of needing a gold medal to win the car is gone: any win counts.
- **Screens:** HUD shows your Blacklist position; the Career tab has the full list with each rival's story, milestone checklist, reward and a GPS button; the result screen shows the rival's line and your new rank; each rival taunts you at the start.
- Old saves keep what they earned (milestones already met are marked silently). The old ten-driver ladder is replaced in the open city (the older Harbor Loop mode is untouched).
- Test: both cities build all 15 rival routes; milestone, unlock, toast and duel-start logic were run headless; the Career screen was rendered to HTML. Not tested: a person actually beating all fifteen — difficulty balance of the new rungs (#15–#11 especially) needs real play.

## Upgrade 33 (parts 2 and 3) — Midnight Burn: the Most Wanted look and feel

Built from the five Need for Speed: Most Wanted videos you supplied. Everything here reuses the existing picture pass and a few sprites, so it adds almost nothing to the cost of a frame (no extra scene drawing, no new heavy effects).

**Look (part 2)**
- **Colour grade per city.** Mumbai: warm amber mid-tones, slightly washed-out colour, harder contrast, heavier dark corners (the gritty Most Wanted street look). Tokyo: crisp and clean, cool neon kept, a touch of warmth, lighter corners.
- **Sun glare.** When the low sun (sunset / dusk) is in front of the camera a warm glow blooms across the screen, like the sun through the trees in the videos. It is weaker in rain and off at night.
- **Speed rush.** The edge-of-screen speed blur starts earlier and is stronger, and stronger again on nitrous.
- **Turn chevrons.** Glowing amber arrow boards stand on the outside of the next sharp corners of your route (up to three at once), as in the videos.
- **HUD in gold.** The speedometer, nitrous bar, map frame, race panel and pop-up messages are restyled in the amber/black Most Wanted style.
- Not done: the rear-view mirror at the top of the screen (it needs the whole city drawn a second time, which would cost speed on weaker laptops), the photo-card result screen, and the phone-style message screens.

**Feel (part 3)**
- **Tokyo Drift:** slides start more easily (handbrake especially), hold while you stay on the throttle and keep their speed, and the car is helped back from a full spin. The chase camera swings further out in a slide.
- **Mumbai:** planted street grip as before, with slightly firmer brakes.
- These handling changes apply to your car only; the computer drivers keep their old, tested handling (a first try that changed them made two of three rivals fail to finish a Tokyo circuit, so it was reverted).
- **Both cities:** the view widens more with speed, the nitrous start gives a sudden wide-angle punch, the camera pulls back further and trembles faintly at speed and on nitrous.

**Tests and limits:** headless software rendering only, so real frame rate on a graphics card was not measured, only that no new passes or scenes were added. Screenshots of Mumbai (golden hour) and Tokyo (dusk) were checked by eye. A scripted drift test confirmed Tokyo slides stay controlled (no spin) and Mumbai's behaviour is unchanged apart from braking. How it feels in a player's hands, and whether the Tokyo slides are too easy or too hard, still needs a human to judge.

## Upgrade 34 — Midnight Burn: a properly Japanese Tokyo, iconic drift roads, Mumbai icons, calendar festivals

**Tokyo now looks and feels like Japan, not like Mumbai**
- **Roads and signals.** Wider roads (expressways 34 m, avenues 24 m, side streets 14.5 m). Traffic signals are the modern Japanese kind: a mast arm reaching over the road, horizontal LED lights with the blue-green "go" light, and a pedestrian signal box. Streets have concrete utility poles with crossarms, transformers and overhead wires.
- **Street furniture.** Drink vending machines and bicycle racks replace the tea stalls, phone booths and hydrants. Grey tile roofs. Residential blocks use a Japanese apartment-wall look (pale walls, balconies and rails).
- **No people at all** in Tokyo (no walkers, no festival crowd).
- **Temples, shrines and gates.** Zojo-ji, Tsurugaoka Hachimangu, Kawasaki Daishi (with a pagoda), Yakuo-in and Hase-dera, each with a courtyard, hall, lanterns and torii gates facing the nearest street. Two vermilion torii tunnels (Nezu and Hikawa) stand over the road and you drive through them.
- **Cherry-blossom parks you drive through.** Avenues at Ueno, Chidorigafuchi, Meguro, Showa Kinen Park and one more. The trees are a new proper tree type in the game's own plant system (`sakura()` in vegetation.js, planted by plants.js along the stretches listed in maps/sakuraAvenue.js): a short dark flared trunk forking into five sweeping limbs, side branches, hundreds of painted flower-cluster cards (new blossom, hanging-sprig and fallen-petal pictures in vegTex.js, built from five-petal flowers with veins, stamens, buds and young bronze leaves), weeping sprigs and a drift of petals on the ground. They lean over the outer lanes so the road becomes a flower tunnel, there are loose groves on the lawns beside each avenue, paper lanterns hang along the pavement, and a light fall of drifting petals appears only near the avenues. Cost per tree: about 1,440 triangles close up, 590 at middle distance, 180 far away (a normal street tree is about 300 close up).
- **Iconic drift races.** *Hakone Turnpike: Midnight Drift* is level 1 and is a third option on the career start screen in Tokyo (Home Garage / Wangan drag / Hakone). *Mount Akina: Downhill Drift* unlocks at level 6 and *Mount Fuji Skyline: The Summit Drift* at level 8. All three are drift-score events over real hairpin roads (16 m wide, bends of roughly 15 to 22 m radius, climbing a few tens of metres).
- Honest placement note: the real Akina and Fuji lie far outside this squeezed map, so their roads stand on the western mountains; the real Hakone falls in the sea of this map, so its road was set in the mountains just inland from that coast.

**Mumbai**
- **New city icons.** Rajabai Clock Tower, Wankhede Stadium (floodlit bowl), Haji Ali Dargah on its own islet with a lit causeway back to the shore, the Colaba Causeway fairy-light bazaar, the Dadar marigold arches (garlands over the lane) and the Aarey banyan tunnel (giant trees meeting over the road). Several are drive-through.
- **Ghat drift races.** *Khandala Ghat: Monsoon Drift* (level 5) and *Igatpuri Ghat: Cloud Drift* (level 7). Igatpuri is a new road of real hairpins (about 26 m radius). The Khandala ghat uses the existing ghat road, whose bends are long sweepers (about 90 m radius) rather than tight hairpins.
- **Festivals follow the calendar.** The festival button is gone; a read-only badge shows what is on, and it changes by itself with the time of year.

**Both cities**
- **Footbridges and subways are only built across a road.** Where a side road merely grazes the pavement at a shallow angle, the old planner could lay a footbridge along it, i.e. in the middle of the traffic. Such spots now stay closed to walkers. Measured the same way before and after: Mumbai had 102 of 486 structures lying along a road (some for 50 m), Tokyo 171 of 796; now Mumbai has 0 of 395 and Tokyo 1 borderline case of 694.
- **Every session opens at dusk** and then moves on by itself: dusk, a slow fade into night, a long night, and the glow returning.

**Bugs found and fixed on the way (these were in the version before)**
- Starting any drift event froze at the "3-2-1" countdown (the code assumed rival cars exist). Fixed; this mattered for every drift race, new and old.
- The map is 86 tiles across but the tile bookkeeping only had room for 64, so tiles in the far south (Colaba, Rajabai and the Pune side in Mumbai; Kamakura and the Hakone area in Tokyo) were mixed up with far-north tiles and dropped right after loading. Fixed everywhere that used the key.

**Tests and limits**
- **Kept light.** The new drive-through decorations (torii tunnels, temples, lanterns, marigold arches, banyan trees) are sorted into 512 m square tiles (`engine/open/tiledGB.js`), and each tile is its own mesh, so the graphics card skips every tile that is off screen instead of drawing the whole map's worth every frame. Trunks, pillars, marigold flowers and hanging strands use cheaper shapes (open 8-sided posts, 5-sided twigs, plain 20-face balls). The cherry trees are not in these tiles: they are ordinary instanced plants (three detail levels, far ones thinned out), see above. Counted by script: all landmark geometry together is about 33,000 triangles in Tokyo and 41,500 in Mumbai; the one map-wide mesh is 18,700 (Tokyo) / 12,800 (Mumbai) triangles, and no tile is larger than about 3,800 (Tokyo) / 18,300 (Mumbai), so only a small slice is on screen at any moment. Worst case for the blossom avenues: about 100 cherry trees in view at close detail is roughly 140,000 triangles, drawn with the same alpha-tested leaf cards as every other tree; the existing automatic picture-quality governor is what protects the frame rate there, and it was not tested on real hardware.
- Headless software rendering only: no real graphics card, nobody actually playing, no ears on the sound. Frame rate on a real laptop was not measured (the triangle counts above are counts, not timings).
- Checked: the three Tokyo touge and two Mumbai ghat events start, put the car on the road facing the right way and let a computer driver run 70 s with no falls (the computer driver does not drift, so scoring itself was only run with that driver); ramp and flyover driving checks on both maps (Tokyo 148 runs, Mumbai 26 runs) had zero stalls, falls or floating; almost no footbridge or subway lies along a road any more (see above); every changed file parses and the whole game screen bundles; screenshots of the temples, torii tunnel, blossom avenues, marigold arches, banyan tunnel and the Mumbai icons were looked at.
- The cherry trees are far more lifelike than the first version (they were pink balls) but they are still painted flower cards on branches inside a game engine, not photographs; close up you can see the cards, and the strong purple dusk lighting tints them lilac on the shaded side. Checked by screenshot only (Ueno, Meguro, Chidorigafuchi, Showa Kinen avenues), and the Mumbai trees were re-checked after the texture sheet grew by one row.
- Not checked: how the new places feel to a person, drift-score balance on the new roads, and the shrine and tower models are simple blocks and balls rather than detailed buildings. The glass office towers in Tokyo still share the generic modern-tower look; the changes above are to the residential blocks, roofs, poles, signals and street furniture.

## Upgrade 35 — "Forgot password" link that never worked: fixed

**What was wrong.** The reset email asked Supabase to send people back to `/auth/callback?next=/reset-password`, but the list of allowed addresses in Supabase (step 4d) only held `/reset-password`. Supabase compares the address letter for letter, so it did not match and Supabase quietly sent people to the home page instead, where nothing happens. On top of that, the old link style only worked in the very same browser that asked for the email (asking on a laptop and opening the email on a phone failed), and email apps that "check" links before you tap them could use the link up. The reset page also sat on "checking your link" forever when anything went wrong.

**What changed in the code**
- **The reset request** (`ForgotPasswordForm.js`, `createResetRequestClient` in `lib/supabaseClient.js`) now sends people back to plain `/reset-password` and asks for the style of link that works in any browser or phone. Friendlier messages for "too many requests" and "no connection".
- **The reset page** (`ResetPasswordForm.js`) understands every kind of link: the usual one (token after the `#`), our own `token_hash` link, the older same-browser `?code=` link, and a typed code from the email (`/reset-password?mode=code`). When a link is used up or expired it says so plainly and offers a new link or the code option, instead of hanging.
- **`/auth/confirm`** no longer uses a reset link up when the page loads; it hands the person to `/reset-password`, and the link is only checked when they press **Save**. (`/auth/callback` also now recognises reset links by what they are, not by a `next=` part.)
- **Safety nets.** A link that lands on the home page with `?code=` or `?token_hash=` is passed on to the right place (`middleware.js`). A link whose token arrives after the `#` on any other page is passed on to `/reset-password` (a tiny script at the top of `app/layout.js`). If Supabase says the link has expired and this browser asked for a reset in the last 3 hours, the person lands on the forgot-password page with an explanation.

**What you must check in Supabase (I cannot do this for you)** — Authentication → URL Configuration
1. **Site URL** = `https://tapandscore.com`
2. **Redirect URLs** contains `https://tapandscore.com/**`

**Strongly recommended: the email template** — Authentication → Emails → Templates → **Reset Password**. This is the one change that stops email apps from using the link up before you tap it, and it makes the link independent of the allowed-address list. Paste this as the message body (Subject: `Reset your Tap & Score password`):

```html
<h2>Reset your password</h2>
<p>Someone asked to reset the password for your Tap &amp; Score account. If that was you, tap the button below to choose a new one. The link works once and stops working after about an hour.</p>
<p><a href="{{ .SiteURL }}/auth/confirm?token_hash={{ .TokenHash }}&type=recovery&next=/reset-password">Choose a new password</a></p>
<p>Link not working? Go to {{ .SiteURL }}/reset-password?mode=code and type your email address and this code: <strong>{{ .Token }}</strong></p>
<p>If you did not ask for this, you can ignore this email.</p>
```

**Honest limits.** Everything was tested with the real Supabase browser library against a pretend Supabase server (13 browser scenarios, 16 server checks, all passing), but not against your live Supabase project, which the build environment cannot reach. Supabase's built-in email sender is limited to a few emails per hour; for a real launch connect your own mail service under Project Settings → Authentication → SMTP.

## Upgrade 36 — Midnight Burn, Mumbai: the Gateway of India in the right place, a sharp zoomable map, a crisper 3D picture

**What was wrong**
- **The Mumbai landmark (Gateway of India) was buried.** It stood about 150 m inland, boxed in by office blocks, and faced the wrong way. It is now planned together with the world: it stands on the waterfront, faces the harbour, has a paved plaza with lamps and a low sea wall in front of it, and the Taj hotel stands opposite it. No building is placed on their ground.
- **The map markers were in the wrong places.** The big map screen still used the scale of an older, smaller world, so cars, shops, sights and pins were all off. Everything is now placed from real metres.
- **The map picture had gone soft.** It was one fixed bitmap stretched to the screen (and rebuilt every time the Map tab opened, which cost speed and memory). It is now drawn from the real road data at whatever zoom you are at.
- **The 3D picture was soft.** The final colour pass blurred the edges of the screen heavily and spent most of its work re-reading the same pixels.

**What changed in the code**
- `midnightburn/engine/open/fullMap.js` (new): the sharp map. Roads, flyovers, tunnels, metro, airports, forest belts and district names are drawn as clean lines. The coast is smoothed. The mouse wheel zooms, dragging moves, and the pictures redraw once the view settles (so moving stays fluid). Buttons: +, −, ME (find my car), ALL.
- `midnightburn/MidnightBurnCity.js`: the Map tab uses it. Sights are drawn where the monument actually stands (the GPS still guides you to the street beside it). The old 3072 px bitmap, which was also built at load, is gone (`maps.js`: `buildBaseMap` and `drawMinimap` removed; the small corner mini-map is unchanged).
- `midnightburn/engine/open/world.js`: a Gateway planner (shore, open water ahead, level ground, reachable street, room for the Taj) and a keep-clear zone for buildings. `landmarks3d.js`: the arch, plaza, sea wall, lamps and the Taj (dome, turrets, lit arcades) follow the plan.
- `midnightburn/engine/fx.js` + `open/render.js`: a gentle sharpening step (stronger when the picture is drawn smaller, off in the two emergency quality steps); the edge softening is milder and starts later; and the colour pass no longer does 8 repeated reads of the picture when you are not driving fast. Measured on the colour pass alone (software test): about half the work in normal driving, and no more than before at high speed.

**Honest limits.** All testing was in a headless browser with software drawing (no real graphics card), so I could not measure real frame rates. I measured the cost of the colour pass relative to before and checked pictures side by side (fine detail measured about 60–70 % higher). The world's random building styles shift a little near the old Gateway spot and elsewhere, which does not affect saved progress (events, shops and sights are generated separately). "Mumbai symbol" was read as the Gateway of India plus the map markers; if you meant something else (for example the logo), tell me which.

## Upgrade 37 — Midnight Burn: separate careers per map, a full road audit of both maps, and Operation Blacksite freeze fixes

### What you asked for
1. Mumbai Drift and Tokyo Drift must be completely separate careers.
2. Both maps should look better again, without making the game slow.
3. Go through every flyover, ramp, corner and junction of both maps (Mumbai especially), drive them, take pictures, and remove the unwanted barriers, edges and inconsistencies.
4. Then: make Operation Blacksite faster and stop its sudden screen freezes.

### 1. Separate careers and separate leaderboards (done, tested 15 of 15 save checks plus a score-route test)
- Each map now has its own save: progress, money, cars, heat, Blacklist, events and settings never mix. A save made before this update is kept for the map it was made on (Mumbai) and Tokyo starts fresh.
- Code: `midnightburn/MidnightBurnCity.js` (`SAVE_KEYS`, `careerKeyOf`, `readCareer`, `loadSave`, `writeSave`, `careerLine`).
- **Leaderboard is now split too, with no database change.** The `scores` table keeps one best score per player per `game` name, and that name is plain text, so Tokyo Drift simply saves under its own name, `midnightburn-tokyo`. The ordinary name `midnightburn` stays the Mumbai board, so every score saved before this is still on the Mumbai board. Code: `lib/boards.js` (new, the list of extra boards and the name helpers), `app/api/scores/route.js` (saves and ranks under the board's name), `games/GameRunner.js` and `midnightburn/MidnightBurnCity.js` (send the map with the score), `components/Leaderboard.js` and `app/leaderboard/page.js` (a Mumbai / Tokyo switch on the board), `lib/achievements.js` and `lib/profile.js` (the two boards still count as one game for "games played", access and activity). The `leaderboard_top` and `user_game_rank` database functions already accept any text, so nothing has to be run in Supabase. Tested with a fake database through the real score route (separate rankings, old scores stay on Mumbai, an unknown board name is ignored).
- **Honest limits:** (1) the Tokyo board starts empty, because nothing records which map an old score came from; all scores saved before this update stay on the Mumbai board. (2) I checked the table definitions in the project (`supabase/schema.sql`, and `migration_001` which removes the old list of allowed game names), not your live Supabase; if the live table has an extra rule on the `game` column, the first Tokyo score would be refused while Mumbai keeps working.

### 2. Looks and speed
- Expansion-joint stripes on the roads were removed (they read as lines across the road), pavements now stop where a crossing road's carriageway begins, and junction patches fill the corners (`textures2.js`, `chunks3d.js`).
- Quality governor (`render.js`): it now tells a slow graphics card (lower the picture sharpness first) from a slow processor (shorter draw distance, fewer plants and people, fewer shadow redraws, in up to three steps), fed by `noteCpu` from `MidnightBurnCity.js`.
- **Honest limit:** all of this was checked in a headless browser with software drawing, so I could not measure real frame rates on a real graphics card.

### 3. Roads, flyovers and junctions (`midnightburn/engine/open/roads.js`)
Roads are built from points every 10 m. Where two roads meet or cross they must be either one flat surface (a level junction) or a flyover with at least 7.4 m clearance. Older passes sometimes turned a small mismatch into a cliff, a wall or a step. What changed:
- **Attached slip roads and T-ends.** A road that starts or ends on another road's carriageway (a slip road, a link, a side street starting on a main road) is a junction and is never lifted into a flyover over it. Before, a street starting on a main road could be lifted 5 m (for example Mumbai `r1069`).
- **Level stays level.** If two roads are level at a crossing they stay level while the lifts of other crossings are made; links are fitted to the roads, never the other way round.
- **Real crossing test.** The "who is over whom" decision uses the true centre-line crossings, and six headroom passes instead of a few.
- **Landings.** An authored road that comes down to the street at its end fades its lifts out over its last 70 m.
- **Steep and clashing links** are re-profiled (`STEEP` 0.14), including links that cut across a third road.
- **Last-mile repair.** After everything else, every crossing still 0.35–3 m out of level is found again and settled together with its neighbours (small local solver).
- **Cliff polish.** Any step steeper than the road ever was (+5 %) is found. The road, and any road that touches it, is re-profiled for about 120 m on each side so the change is spread out; every level crossing stays level, every flyover keeps its headroom, nothing sinks below the ground, and no step may be steeper than the road's own steepest original step (+2 %). If a first try cannot be solved it is retried over a wider stretch (up to 3 times), and a last clean-up try only removes cliffs.
- **Low-flyover top-up (new).** Every place where one road passes over another with less than the full 7.4 m clearance (and the two are not level anywhere else) is found. The two roads are re-profiled together with their neighbours until the clearance is 7.7 m. If the crossing is at a road's end (a road starting or ending on the other road) the two are made level instead, unless that end is already level with a third road, in which case the flyover clearance is used.
- **Side-by-side roads and low decks (new).** Every pair of roads that lie on top of each other for a stretch (3 or more checks across the road width, 0.8 m or more apart in height) is found. Two ground-level roads, or two decks, are tied to the same height; a deck over a ground-level road is lifted to at least 5 m above it. Road ends are left alone, because a road that starts by climbing out of a street cannot have 5 m of room at its very start.
- Solver speed: only the points that may move are visited, the rules are prepared once, and later tries re-solve only the pairs still unresolved. Cost: about 8–10 s of load time in the (slow, 2-core) test machine instead of 1–3 s, roughly 5–8 % more world-build time there. I could not time a real phone or PC.
- The road graph (used by the map, GPS and AI) is rebuilt from the final heights so it only joins roads that really meet.

**Measured, before and after (same checks, same test car; "before" is the task-start build, except where noted):**
| | Mumbai | Tokyo |
|---|---|---|
| Roads with a step steeper than 0.15 m between two 10 m points | 11 → 0 | 12 → 2 (the two left are the Aqua-Line and Rainbow road starts, which climb out of street level on purpose) |
| Junction drive-through (every junction approached from every arm, about 45,000 and 42,600 runs): runs that hit something or got stuck | 643 → 64 | 641 → 83 |
| Whole-road drive (every road end to end, 3,945 and 5,521 runs): flagged runs | 2 | 18 (all of them tiny height bumps; no crash, no stuck, no fall) |
| Pairs of roads that lie on top of each other for 1 m or more at different heights | 163 → 32 | 328 → 88 |
| ... of those, long stretches (about 80 m or more) | 9 | 25 |
| ... of those, 2 m or more apart | 7 | 27 |
| Flyovers with less than the full clearance (stricter count: anything from 2.9 to 7.36 m over a road it is not level with; "before" is the previous delivery) | 18 → 1 | 21 → 5 |
| Crossings that are neither level nor a flyover (0.6–4 m apart; previous delivery: 0 and 0) | 1 | 6 |
| Ramp mouth test (aimed at each ramp from different sides, no traffic), same 26 ramps in both builds | 91 → 95 problem runs of 234 (no real change) | not run (this test drives Mumbai's ramps only) |

Most of the junction runs that are still flagged are the car meeting a dead-end barricade or a roadside prop, which is meant to stop the car; they are not holes or walls in the road.

**The six limits from the previous delivery, and what happened to each.**
1. *Leaderboard shared by both maps.* **Fixed.** See section 1.
2. *Twin roads at slightly different heights (21 pairs in Mumbai, 34 in Tokyo).* **Improved, not gone.** The pairs the solver flagged went 42 → 6 (Mumbai) and 87 → 14 (Tokyo). The independent check above (which also counts short patches and every road edge) shows 32 and 88 pairs of 1 m or more, down from 163 and 328 at the task start. The longest left are: Mumbai `r1031`/`r1128` (about 2.5 m, 360 m long), `efree`/`r454`, `r79`/`r245`; Tokyo `r390`/`r493`, `r383`/`r479`, `r388`/`r490`, `r7`/`wangan` (2.3–2.5 m, 600 m to 1.2 km). Several of these are expressway starts that climb out of a street beside them (for example `k1`), where the height difference is the climb itself; I did not check every pair one by one. Pictures of `r7`/`wangan` show two clean parallel decks at slightly different heights.
3. *Low flyovers (14 per map at 4.3–7 m).* **Fixed in Mumbai, nearly in Tokyo.** Mumbai: one left, `eeh` over `r317`, 7.24 m instead of 7.4 m. Tokyo: five left, of which four are within 0.05 m of the target (7.35–7.39 m); the real one is `k1` over `r1269` at 3.2 m (an expressway start).
4. *Low decks (about 2.4 m) over a street.* **Mostly fixed.** Left: Mumbai `efree`/`r454` and `puneyring`/`r1037` (about 2.2 m), Tokyo `kk16`/`r93`, `kk246`/`gaikan`, `r390`/`r493`, `k1`/`r1269`. They are the same expressway-start cases as above.
5. *Ramp mouth test: 3 of 306 runs "slide into the water".* **Traced; not caused by this work.** I was wrong about the water. In the game the "fell off, put me back on the road" flag fires for the sea and also for any drop of more than 5.5 m off a flyover (`drive.js`); here it was the drop, and the car comes to rest at about 9–10 m height, not in the sea. All falls are on one ramp, `ramp_weh_18` (4 of 306 runs now), and the task-start build falls in the same places (4 runs: three on `weh_18`, including one aimed dead straight at speed 48, and one on `atal_582`). So this is not caused by this work. The test car follows a fixed line at a fixed speed instead of steering, and I believe that is why it leaves the ramp's edge, but I did not prove that a steering player can never fall there.
6. *Drift-race test: car stops near the start.* **Traced; not caused by the roads.** The test car follows one road only. The Ghat race starts on the last two points of its road (nowhere to go), and the Colaba race starts about 100 m before the end of its road. The stops are at the same spots in the task-start build (Colaba about 6122,17668; Ghat about 17435,9946). My earlier explanation (a bench now 4 m from the road edge) was wrong. Events, shops and saved progress are generated separately and do not move.

**New honest limits.**
- Seven crossings (1 in Mumbai, 6 in Tokyo) are now 0.7–3.2 m out of level where the previous delivery had none: Mumbai `hbr`/`r317` (0.84 m, under the `eeh` flyover); Tokyo `k1`/`r1269` (3.2 m), `gaikan`/`kk246` (2.8 m), `r1269`/`r1376` (1.5 m), `r1160`/`r1185` (1.1 m), `yokosuka`/`r1307` (0.9 m), `miura`/`r1370` (0.7 m). They are drivable steps or gentle slopes, not walls, but they are not clean.
- The ramp comparison is not a win: on the same 26 ramps the numbers are 91 before and 95 after (234 runs). Four more ramps are now detected (34 ramps, 306 runs, 133 problem runs), and I did not look at those four on the task-start build.
- Tests are numeric (driving every road and junction with a test car) plus pictures (angled and straight-down) at 7 changed spots in Mumbai and 9 in Tokyo, with no page errors; the flyovers, decks and crossings I looked at join cleanly. I have not played every metre by hand. Everything ran in a headless browser with software drawing, so there is no real frame-rate figure.

### 4. Operation Blacksite: sudden freezes (done and measured)
**What caused the freezes.** The 3D engine builds a "program" for the graphics card for every distinct lighting set-up. Every explosion, smoke cloud and the bomb added or removed a light, so the engine rebuilt around 29 programs per explosion, each taking tens of milliseconds, and the half-time/round change built soldier models again from scratch. Sound effects also piled up when many events happened at once.
**What changed** (`blacksite/engine/*`):
- A constant number of lights: explosion and smoke lights come from a fixed pool, the bomb has a permanent light that is just switched on and off, so the card never recompiles (`fx.js`, `engine.js`).
- A warm-up compiles every program once while the match loads (`renderer.compile`, `warmShaders`), and soldier models are built once per side (`prebuildModels`) instead of at every round.
- Quality changes by the automatic governor wait until a round is frozen or ended, never in the middle of play.
- Sounds far away or already too many are skipped (`sfx.js`, `audible()`); painting canvases use CPU-backed canvases (`cpucanvas.js`) so repeated texture reads are cheap; the 3D context is released when leaving the game (`forceContextLoss`).
**Measured (headless, software drawing):** program rebuilds per explosion about 29 → 0; the longest stall at half-time more than 1 s → tens of milliseconds.
**Honest limit:** measured without a real graphics card; real hardware should be better, but I could not time it.

## Upgrade 38 — Midnight Burn: heavy car customisation, hidden garages that gate your level, and outrun races

### What you asked for
1. Heavy car customisation in Midnight Burn, for performance and for looks.
2. Lots of hidden garages on the map that are not on the GPS, and the driver must find a few of them to reach the next level (like NFS Most Wanted and Underground 2).
3. Outrun races against other racers.

### 1. Car customisation (done; pictures checked, maths checked)
**Performance — nine parts per car** (`engine/physics.js`, driving code in `engine/open/drive.js`). Every car keeps its own set. The three old lines (engine, tyres, nitrous) are joined by six new ones, and each new tier really changes how the car drives:
| Part | Tiers | What it does | Driver level needed for tiers 1, 2, 3, 4 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Engine | 5 | +2.4 % top speed and +9 % pull per tier | none |
| Turbocharger | 4 | +1.2 % top speed, +5 % pull per tier | 2, 4, 6, 8 |
| Gearbox | 4 | gear changes take 0.027 s less per tier and lose less power; +1.5 % pull | 1, 3, 5, 7 |
| Tyres & suspension | 5 | +6 % grip, +3.5 % steering per tier | none |
| Brakes | 4 | +8 % braking force per tier | 1, 2, 4, 6 |
| Weight reduction | 4 | +3 % pull, +2 % steering, +1 % grip per tier | 2, 4, 6, 8 |
| Aero & downforce | 3 | +3.5 % grip, stays planted at speed (a little less top speed) | 3, 5, 8 |
| Differential | 3 | slides keep their speed; better in the wet | 2, 4, 6 |
| Nitrous | 5 | +9 % kick, 15 % less drain per tier | none |
Prices rise with the value of the car. The garage shows every part with its tier, price, what it gives and the level it needs, plus stat bars, a class letter (D to S+) and a rating that include the new parts.

**Looks** (`engine/cars.js`, `engine/carParts.js`, `engine/scene.js`, `MidnightBurnGarage.js`): a tabbed Customise panel with a live car on the turntable. Paint: any colour (sliders) or 10 finishes (gloss, primer, metallic, pearl, matte, candy, satin, chameleon, carbon, chrome). 11 liveries (stripes, side, racing, checker, hex, speed, split, flames, camo, grid, rally) each in 12 colours. Wheels: 10 styles × 4 sizes × 12 colours, 9 brake-caliper colours, 4 camber settings, 5 ride heights/stances (stock, sport, low, slam, lift). Body: 6 kits, 7 wings, 4 bonnets, 5 exhausts, 4 roof treatments, 3 mirror finishes, 5 headlight tints, 7 window tints, 10 underglow colours, and your racing number.
- Old saves: any option a saved car does not have falls back to the stock look, and any part not bought is tier 0, so every old car looks and drives exactly as before.

### 2. Hidden garages (done; Mumbai and Tokyo both tested)
- **30 garages on each map** (`engine/open/garages.js`, planned when the map loads, always the same places). Each is a small workshop beside a street, with its door facing the road, never on a road, a building, a lamp, a tree, a shop, an event start or a landmark, on flat dry ground and at least 140 m (in practice about 800 m) from the next one.
- **Not on the map, the minimap, the GPS or the full-screen map** until found. You find one by driving within 30 m. A faint purple "hidden garage nearby" signal with a warmer/colder bar appears within about 130 m. The Career tab lists the closest unfound ones as rumours (a landmark or district clue, no pin).
- **Found garages** light up (neon frame, sign, light on the pavement), get a map marker and a beam, can be fast-travelled to, and open a full workshop (performance, looks and cars) like any other garage. A find pays ₹250 / ¥250 and 80 XP. The buildings are solid for you and for traffic.
- **They gate the level.** The driver's level stops at the highest one the garages found allow, however much XP you have: level 2 needs 2 garages, level 3 needs 4, then 6, 8, 10, 12, 14, 17 and 20 for level 10. When you have the XP but not the garages the game says "LEVEL N IS READY: FIND K MORE HIDDEN GARAGES" and the HUD shows the count. Level-ups, car-lot unlocks and part tiers follow the level, so they wait too.
- **Old careers** keep the level they had already reached (`career.lvFloor`); only the levels above it wait for garages. The free/Classic version has no garages and no gate.
- Code: `sim.js` (`garageScan`, `foundGarage`, `G.level`, `G.garageProgress`), `render.js` (`syncGarages`), `MidnightBurnCity.js` (HUD, Career tab, map).

### 3. Outrun races (done; headless tests on both maps, on-screen parts rendered)
- **Street racers.** Eight named racers per city (own names, cars and looks; different from the Blacklist rivals) cruise the roads at night: three STREET, three PRO, two ACE. Tiers arrive with your level (PRO from level 3, ACE from level 6), one racer on the road at a time, two from level 4. They appear ahead of you and cruise away at roughly 90–135 km/h (depending on the car), pull out and overtake slow traffic, and show as a pink dot on the minimap within about 330 m; a hint at the top of the screen gives their name, distance and side. A racer stuck behind traffic for 8 s is removed and another appears.
- **Challenge.** Pull up beside one (within 40 m) and press ENTER or L. The racer answers with a line. A 3-2-1 runs while both cars keep driving, then it is on. No start line, no checkpoints: the GPS shows the racer's route.
- **Rules.** Open up a **220 m lead** and hold it for 2.5 s to win; fall 220 m behind and hold it and you lose. If nobody gets there, whoever is in front when the route ends (about 2–6 km) or after 5 minutes wins. The lead is measured along the racer's route, so a smart shortcut counts. The racer eases off when it is ahead and pushes when behind, so races stay close. A bar at the top shows who is ahead, your lead in metres, the hold timer and the time left. X gives up (counts as a loss). Pausing, the map and fast travel work as in any event (fast travel is off during the race).
- **Strength** scales with your level and the racer's tier (car, tuning and driving skill all rise). ACE racers drive hero cars, so beating one takes a strong car of your own. A racer whose car is better than yours drives a little gentler (and a worse one a little harder), so the tier, not the car it happens to own, sets the challenge. **Rewards:** cash and XP grow with level and tier, +10 % per win in a row (up to +50 %) and +60 % for the first time you beat each racer; a loss gives a little XP. Wins also count towards the career score (120 each, first 100 only; 250 for each racer beaten; 100 per garage found).
- Career tab: record (won, lost, streak, best streak) and the eight racers with "beaten ×n" and the level they appear at. The record is saved with the career (`career.outruns`); old saves start with an empty one.
- Code: `engine/open/outrun.js` (roster, difficulty, rewards, route helpers), `sim.js` (spawn, `startOutrun`, `stepOutrun`, `finishOutrun`), `render.js` and `maps.js` (cars and minimap dot), `MidnightBurnCity.js` (prompt, lead bar, result card, Career tab, controls help).

### Tests (headless, software drawing; "ALL PASS" means every line passed)
**Hidden garages** (`ramp/entry_GAR.js`): Mumbai 30 of 30 checks and Tokyo 30 of 30 checks pass. 30 garages on each map; none overlaps a building, lamp, tree, road or shop; all on flat dry ground; each door is 4.7–11.2 m from a road at the garage's own level (not a flyover above it); the closest two are 588 m apart (Mumbai) and 793 m (Tokyo). Rules: a new career is level 1 and held there with 99,999 XP; the hint appears within 130 m and not at 250 m; finding the garages one by one gives exactly the level table (30 steps checked), the same garage twice counts once, all found gives level 10; found garages join the shop list and can be entered; an old level-6 career stays at 6, needs exactly 12 garages for level 7; the free version is not gated.
**Outruns** (`ramp/entry_OUT.js`): 58 of 58 checks pass on Mumbai and 58 of 58 on Tokyo. Roster: 8 racers, 3 tiers, every car and every look option exists. Difficulty and rewards rise with level, tier, streak and first win; route helpers; long routes can be found from most places. In the game: a racer appears while roaming, on a road, cruising at a sensible speed and still driving after 15 s; the challenge is offered alongside and not from 120 m; starting works, the racer joins the race, lights count down, the race goes live; a standing player loses in about 7–10 s; a scripted traffic-aware driver wins a street-tier outrun (after 71 s with a 136 m lead on Mumbai and 239 m on Tokyo); win/loss records, streak, cash, XP and toast are right; giving up counts as a loss; the car stays where it is after the result; the beaten racer is not back at once; a normal race clears the racers; racers pause in the shop; the score adds 100 per garage, 120 per win (first 100) and 250 per racer beaten; an old save, a partial record and a broken record all load; the free version also has racers.
**Screens** (`ramp/entry_OUI.js`, drawn to HTML text): 18 of 18 checks pass: Career tab section and numbers, racer hint (ahead, behind, left, right), challenge prompt (and a shop prompt wins over it), lead bar when ahead, behind, holding and losing the gap, lights countdown, win card and loss card.
**Difficulty check** (`ramp/entry_OC.js`): 21 scripted outruns (levels 1, 3, 6 and 9; street, pro and ace racers; three driver skills each), always with a plain Kestrel tuned like a driver of that level. Street racers at level 1 and 3 (a car like yours): 5 of 6 won, close races of 83–108 s with average speeds within 1 %. Pro racer at level 3: 3 of 3 won. Pro at level 6: 1 of 3; ace at level 6: 0 of 3; pro at level 9: 0 of 3; ace at level 9: 1 of 3. So the plain Kestrel stops being enough from about level 6, which is when the hero cars arrive. The test driver is not a person, so read it as a direction and not as a promise.
**Pictures** (headless browser): three Mumbai and three Tokyo garages, unfound (dim) and found (lit) and from a distance, and a picture of each of the 16 street racers' cars in the game world.

### Honest limits (new)
- **Balance has not been played by a person.** I tuned the outrun difficulty with a scripted test driver at three skill levels, per level and tier (it won easy outruns and lost to ACE racers at high level, and the results swing with traffic), but I could not judge how it feels to a human hand. If races are too easy or too hard, the numbers to change are in `difficulty()` and `OUT` in `outrun.js`.
- **The racers' cars and the garage buildings are simple.** The garages are boxes with a shutter, sign and light (no interior); entering opens the same workshop menu as any garage. A few garages sit partly behind roadside trees; that is by design ("hidden") but the 30 m find radius still finds them.
- **Stance and camber are looks only.** They change how the car sits and the wheel angle, not the physics.
- **Racers sometimes get held up by traffic** for a few seconds (they overtake, but not perfectly); one that stays blocked for 8 s is removed and another appears. During an outrun a rival that sticks is moved forward after 4 s.
- **No sound of its own** for the outrun and no radio chatter (added in Upgrade 39, below); no multiplayer, the racers are computer drivers.
- **Everything was tested in a headless browser with software drawing**, so I have no real frame-rate figures for the new parts.

## Upgrade 39 — Midnight Burn: sharper car bodies, a stronger sense of speed, and racers that talk

### What you asked for
1. Better car shapes: "the cars are built from simple shapes", and detailed car models would need free-licence 3D files.
2. Make speed feel fast, the way Most Wanted does: the camera pulling back, blurred screen edges, wind noise and a big kick when you hit nitrous; make the existing sound punchier.
3. Give the outruns their own sounds and taunts from the racers.

### 0. Two corrections to what I said earlier (read this first)
- **The cars were not "simple shapes".** They were already smooth, lofted bodies (cross-sections along the car, cut-out wheel arches, creases, lamps, plate), not boxes. I overstated the gap when I wrote the list of improvements. What was true: every tail and nose ended in a soft rounded point, so each coupe read like a smooth pill. That is what this upgrade changes.
- **Some of the speed feel already existed** (a wider view at speed, some edge blur, wind noise, a nitrous roar). I strengthened those and added the missing parts rather than building them from scratch. Also: **the view-widening effect had never worked.** Its line in `render.js` sat on the end of a `//` comment, so the code after it never ran. It is fixed (it is now two lines), so the view really does widen with speed and punches wider when the nitrous hits.

### 1. Car shapes (done; looked at in the garage and in the game)
- **Free 3D models: not used, and why.** The sources I could reach from here were not reachable, the one sample car I could find has a licence that does not clearly allow commercial use, a real-brand car carries trademark risk, a fixed mesh would break every customisation part (wings, kits, bonnets, rims, stance, paint finishes all attach to the generated body), and detailed models are typically several MB each, multiplied by every car in the game. Kept the generated bodies and improved them.
- **What changed** (`engine/cars.js`, end of the ARCH tables, `kammTail`, `wideNose`, `crisp`): every closed-body car now has a **flat, tall rear face** (a "Kamm tail") with tight corners, which is where the lamps, plate, diffuser and exhausts sit; a **blunter, wider nose** with a flat leading edge instead of the pointed wedge; and **squarer shoulders** with a deeper shoulder crease and sill groove so light breaks along a line. Squareness is now set per station along the body (`loft` takes an optional fifth number per station), and each car's numbers come from its own first and last stations, so every car keeps its own width, height and character. Lamps and plates needed a fallback (`skinAt` end-face) because the flat face has no curved surface to sit on.
- **Not changed:** open-wheel cars (frame, praga and the like) and the traffic cars in `open/vehicles3d.js`.
- Customisation still fits: a GT wing, attack kit, quad tips and a scoop were checked on the new tails (Kestrel, Phantom, Brute).

### 2. Speed feel (done; pictures checked)
- **Camera** (`engine/open/render.js` `updateCamera`): it falls back when the car surges (smoothed acceleration), is thrown back on the nitrous kick, sits a little lower at speed, shakes more, and has a small cockpit buffet. The field of view widens by up to 11° with speed, 7° more on nitrous, and punches wider for the first moment of a nitrous hit (`P.kick`).
- **Screen** (`engine/fx.js`): the edge blur is stronger and ramps in faster than it eases out; colour fringing rises on nitrous; new **speed streaks** (thin pale lines rushing out from the middle and showing only near the edges) and a brief **blue flash from the edges** on the nitrous kick. After the first test the kick washed the picture out, so the flash and blur were reduced before this was finished.
- **Sound** (`engine/engineAudio.js`): a heavy low rush of air that grows with speed (cockpit view is quieter), a buffeting flutter, a louder nitrous slam (valve crack, sub-bass boom, rising whoosh, blow-off hiss, exhaust crackle) and a puff and drop when it runs dry. The engine, music and radio are untouched.

### 3. Racers that talk, with sounds (done; headless tests on both maps)
- **Radio chatter** (`engine/open/outrun.js` `CHAT`, `chatLine`): every one of the 16 racers has their own lines for seven moments: when they first notice you, the start, when they are ahead, when you are ahead, a close race, when you crash and when they use nitrous. Lines are short (at most 90 characters), never the same line twice in a row, and have a generic fallback. Mumbai racers talk in Mumbai street slang (Hindi and English mixed); each Tokyo racer talks in the style of their nickname (the night bus, the cat, the fox).
- **When they speak** (`engine/open/sim.js` `say`, `stepOutrun`): at most one line every few seconds. The challenge line is the racer's existing taunt, now shown as a radio message. A separate random generator drives the chatter, so talking never changes how the world or the race plays out. The result card keeps the racer's win/lose line.
- **On screen** (`MidnightBurnCity.js`): a small radio box at the top right (under the district name, clear of the map and speedometer) with the racer's name, tag and line; it fades after a few seconds and steps aside for the result card.
- **Sounds** (`engineAudio.js` `cue(name)`): a radio squelch before each line; the racer's engine blips when it first notices you; a two-blip rev on the challenge; countdown beeps (three, then a higher "go" with a thump); a rising chime when you take the lead and a falling one when you lose it; a win fanfare and a lose sting; and a **second engine voice for the racer** that sits left or right of you, gets louder as it comes alongside and bends in pitch as it passes. Forfeiting also plays the lose sting.
- **Not added:** spoken voices (the lines are text).

### Tests (headless, software drawing; "ALL PASS" means every line passed)
- **Outruns** (`ramp/entry_OUT.js`): 71 of 71 on Mumbai and 71 of 71 on Tokyo (was 58; 13 new). New checks: every racer has a short, non-repeating line for every moment; an unknown moment still gets a line; pulling alongside makes the racer rev and speak; the taunt arrives as a radio message with the challenge sound; the lights give three beeps and one go sound in order; the racer speaks at the start; in the test race the lines were never closer than 2 s and there were never more than 14; losing, winning and forfeiting each play the right sound; lines fade after a few seconds; the game works with no sound hook set.
- **Screens** (`ramp/entry_OUI.js`): 22 of 22 (was 18): the radio box shows who is talking and what they say, is absent when nobody speaks, steps aside for the result card, and shows during a race.
- **Hidden garages** (`ramp/entry_GAR.js`): still 30 of 30 on Mumbai and 30 of 30 on Tokyo.
- **Sound** (headless Chromium with an analyser on the output, no exceptions on any path): idle, cruise, fast and nitrous levels are within a few per cent of the old engine's, muted is silent, every cue plays, and each beep or chime stands 6 to 23 dB above the engine in its own pitch (they were 3 to 6 dB, too quiet, before I raised them).
- **Pictures** (headless browser): the speed look at 50 m/s, on nitrous and at the kick, on both maps; before/after of Kestrel, Phantom and Brute from the front and rear; racers' cars in the real scene; the new radio box over a game picture.

### Honest limits (new)
- **Nothing here has been played or listened to by a person.** I checked the pictures, the numbers and the sound levels, not the feel. Speed feel in particular depends on frame rate and on your own eyes and speakers.
- **No real frame rate.** All pictures came from software drawing. The new screen effects use no extra picture reads, and the speed streaks are skipped on the lightest quality setting, but I have no figures from a real graphics card.
- **The car change is real but moderate.** Tails and noses are crisper and flatter; the cars are still generated shapes and will not match a modelled PC-game car (fine detail such as interiors, mirrors and panel gaps is still basic). Open-wheel cars and traffic cars are unchanged. Brute's nose is now chunkier than I would like.
- **The racers' voices are text and sound effects only.** There is no spoken speech.
- **The sound level of the engine overall barely changed**, because the output is compressed to a steady loudness; the new wind and nitrous work shows up as texture and as the nitrous punch, not as a louder game.
- **Sound effects not heard by a person.** The beeps, chimes and rival engine are made from simple tones and noise; they are measured but not judged by ear.
